step.   Although  the  original  MMA  method  is  designed  for  MF-DFA,  it  can  obviously be  applied  to  other  methods  of
multifractal analysis.
     The MMA method has been applied to investigate the multifractal nature of daily returns of the Dow Jones Indus-
trial  Average  (DJIA),  New  York  Stock  Exchange  Index (NYSE),  S&P  500  Index,  Heng  Seng  Index (HSI),  Shanghai
Stock  Exchange  Composite  Index  and  Shenzhen  Stock  Exchange  Component  Index  (SZCI)  from  12  May  1992  to  8
May  8  2012  [327,       328]  and  nine  stock  market  indices  (CAC  40,  DAX,  FTSE  100,  HSI,  NIKKEI  225,  SSEC,  NAS-
DAQ, S&P  500) from 3 January 2000 to 1 October 2014 [329].
     More generally, this method is equivalent to calculate the local logarithmic slopes of the  Fq(s)  functions:

                                                                h(q, s)=   lim      d ln Fq(s′)

                                                                             s′→s      d ln s′ ,                                                                                                                                 (142)
which is widely utilized in the study of structure functions in turbulence [226,                             229], as well as in DFA [330].  The idea
to  estimate  local  logarithmic  slopes  is  especially  informative  when  the  scaling  range  is  narrow  and  it  can  serve  as  a
method for the  determination of  scaling ranges.  Different numerical methods can  be adopted to estimate  numerically
the local logarithmic slopes H(q, s) and the linear ﬁtting in a moving window suggested in the MMA    method is one of
them.   Empirical  analyses  have  been  performed on  ﬁnancial  ti  me  series,  such  as  the  WTI  crude  oil  market  [331]  and
stock market indices [332], without referring to the  name of multiscale multifractal analysis.

2.5.5.   Time-varying multifractal analysis
     To  quantify  the  evolution  of  a  multifractal  spectrum,  one  can  perform  a  multifractal  analysis  of  time  series  in
moving  windows  or  rolling  windows.    We  would  like  to  stress  that  short  window  sizes  will  reduce  the  estimation
accuracy  of  multifractal  quantities  and  increase  the  uncertainty  of  the  results.    Time-varying  multifractal  quantities
have practical implications, as discussed in Section                    8.2.4.
     Alternatively, Xiong and Zhang proposed the  time-varying multifractal spectrum distribution (TM-MFSD) [333].
The key idea is to  perform multifractal analysis on the instantaneous autocorrelation function

                                                         r(i,δ)= E[X∗(i−∆/2)X(i+∆/2)],                                                                                                                 (143)

rather  than  the  raw  time  series  X(i),  where  the  time-delayed  conjugation  X∗(i)  of  the  analyzed  series  X(i)  is  selected
as the  window  function.  To  obtain the  time-varying multifractal spectrum distribution of  a  time  series,  one  can  adopt
different  multifractal  analysis  methods  described  in  previous  sections,  such  as  WTMM  [333],  wavelet  leaders  [334,
335],  MF-DMA [336],  and MF-DFA  [337].  Essentially, the  multifractal analysis is performed with  these  methods on
moving windows.

2.5.6.   Phase space reconstruction
     A  classic  method  in  nonlinear dynamics  is  to  embed  properly the  time  series  into  a  phase  space  and  estimate  the
generalized dimensions Dq  of the data points in the phase space [128,                           148,   149,   338], which is however less applied in
econophysics.  Based  on  the  delay  embedding technique  [339],  the  time  series  X(i)  can  be  converted into  a  sequence
of vectors:              −→
                                       X( j)=  [X( j), X( j+τ),···, X( j+ (d− 1)τ)],      j=  1,···, Nv,                                                                        (144)
                                                                                33
where   Nv   is  the  number  of  points  (or  vectors)  in  the  phase  space,τ  is  a  suitably  chosen  time  delay,   and  d   is  the
embedding dimension of the phase space.  The embedding dimension d  and the delayτ can be determined in different
ways [149,       340–   344].
     The relative number of data points within a  distance R to  point i  is calculated by

                                                                             Nv∑       (      ⏐⏐⏐⏐−→             ⏐⏐⏐⏐)
                                                         ci(s)=     1Nv            H     s−     X(i)−−→X( j)        ,                                                                                                          (145)
                                                                           j=1, j/nequali

whereH  is the  Heaviside function.  The generalized correlation integral Cq(s)  is [345]
                                                                            Nv∑               1/(q−1)
                                                             Cq(s)=           1        [ci(s)]q−1               ,                                                                                                                  (146)
                                                                             Nv     i

which is the generalization of the correlation integral [128,                           148,    149].  The generalized dimensions are given by [338]

                                                                        Dq≡  lim       log Cq(s)
                                                                                 s→0      log s .                                                                                                                                          (147)

     Lee  investigated  the  multifractal  characteristics  of  the  1-min  volatility  of  the  Korea  composite  stock  price  index
KOSPI  from  20  March  1992  to  28  February  2007  [346].   The  volatility  time  series  is  ﬁrst  detrended  with  a  wavele  t
ﬁlter and then a  multifractal analysis in  the phase space wit  hτ=  1  and d=  1  is performed to conﬁrm the  presence of
multifractality.

2.5.7.   Asymmetric  multifractal analysis
     Inspired  by  the  stylized  fact  that  the  correlation  between  two  assets  is  usually  asymmetric  [347–                                     350],  Alvarez-
Ramirez et al.  developed the asymmetric multifractal detrended ﬂuctuation analysis (A-MF-DFA) to detect asymmet-
ric  multifractal  scaling  in  individual  time  series  [351].   In  this  method,  the  trend  function  is  linear  such  that  ai=   0
for  all  i>  1  in  Eq.  (116).   The  main  idea  is  to  calculate  the  upwards  (downwards) ﬂuct  uation  functions in  which  the
removed linear  trends have  non-negative (negative) slopes.   Mathematically, the  qth-order upwards overall  detrended
ﬂuctuation is
                                                                        [   1+ kv          ]    q1
                                                                1         2Ns∑                         q
                                           F+q (s)=       ∑2Ns    1+kv              2        Fv(s)        =⟨[Fv(s)]q⟩|a1≥0,                                                                          (148)
                                                             v=1    2     v=1
and the qth-order downwards overall detrended ﬂuctuation is

                                                                     F−q (s)=⟨[Fv(s)]q⟩|a1<0,                                                                                                                                   (149)

where kv=  1 if the slope of the linear trend (116) is nonnegative, that is a1≥  0, and kv=−1 if the slope a1<  0.  When
q=  0, we  apply L’Hˆospital’s rule as in Eq. (                  111).  The respective generalized Hurst  exponents can be determined by

                                                                            F±q (s)∼  sH±(q).                                                                                                                                                  (150)

Note that the original A-MF-DFA method uses the mean absolute value of the  residuals in  each segment v [351].
     Empirical studies using  the  A-MF-DFA  method investigated and  conﬁrmed the  asymmetric multifractal behavior
in  the  daily  returns of  the  DJIA  index from 23 May  1980 to  25  August  2008 [352],  the  WTI  oil  price  from 2  January
1986 to 14 December 2010 [352], the SSEC index from 19 December 1990 to 27 April 2012 and the SZCI index from
2  April  1991  to  27  April  2012  [353],  and  the  DJIA,  NASDAQ,  NYSE,  and  S&P500  indices  from  1  January  1991  to
31 December 2015 [354].
     The  A-MF-DFA  method  can  be  easily  extended  to  design  the  asymmetric  multifractal  detrending  moving  aver-
age  analysis  (A-MF-DMA),  in  which  moving  averages  serve  as  the  trend  function  and  the  direction  (or  “sign”)  is
determined exactly as  for the  A-MF-DFA method [355].  The asymmetric multiscale detrended ﬂuctuation analysi  s is
obtained  by  calculating  the  local  exponents at  different  time  scales,  which  has  been  applied  to  investigate  the  hourly

                                                                                     34
returns of the California electricity spot prices during years 1999 and 2000 [356].
    Cao   et  al.     developed  the   asymmetric  multifractal  detrended  cross-correlation  analysis   (MF-ADCCA)   method
[357],   as  an  asymmetry  extension  of  the  MF-X-DFA  method  (see  Section                        3.4).     The  method  has  been  applied  to
identify  asymmetric  cross-correlations  between  the  daily  returns  of  the  SSEC  index  and  six  foreign  exchange  rates
from 22 July 2005 to 13 January 2012 [357], the  5-min returns of the CSI  300 index spot  and futures returns from 30
June 2011 to 7 June 2013 [358], the daily returns of the gold spot prices in the Shanghai Gold Exchange (Au 99.95) and
the  New  York  Gold  Exchange from 2  January  2003 to  27  April  2012  [359],  the  EU  ETS  for  carbon emission  trading
products vs.  energy futures –  Brent  crude oil  (Brent),  Richa  rds bay  coal  (Coal),  UK  base  electricity  (Electricity) and
UK  natural  gas  (Gas)  –  from  1  January  2008  to  31  December  2012  [                  360],  the  daily  returns  of  the  WTI  price  and
eight foreign exchange rates  (CAD,  MXN,  NOK,  GBP,  JPY,  AUD,  EUR  and KRW)  against the  USD  from 4  January
2000  to  31  December  2014  [361],  the  Shanghai  SSEC  and  four  other  indices  (US  S&P  500,  Germany  DAX,  India
BSESN,  and Brazil  BVSP)  from 1 January 2002 to 26 September 2014 [362],  and the  WTI crude oil prices and eight
stock  merket  indices  (Brazil  IBOVESPA,  Chile  IPSA,  Argentina  MERVAL,  Mexico  IPC,  Colombia  COLCAP,  Peru
SPBVL, S&P500 and DJIA)  from the 28 January 2005 to  29 December 2016 [363].
    Chen  and  Zheng  proposed  the  asymmetric  joint  multifractal  analysis  based  on  partition  functions  [364],   as  an
extension of  the  MF-X-PF  method (see  Section                3.1).  This  extension becomes feasible  for ﬁnancial  volatility    because
one can use  the associated returns to deﬁne the signs in the se  gments.

2.5.8.   Multifractal diffusion entropy analysis
    The diffusion entropy analysis (DEA) for time  series{X(i)}Ni=1  is based on the  Shannon entropy [365–                                 368], which
can  be  determined  by  taking  asset  prices  as  an  example.   For  a  given  diffusion  time  or  time  scale  s,  we  calculate  the
return series
                                              ∆X(i, s)=  X(i)− X(i−  s),     i=  1, 2,···, N−  s,
which  denotes  the  total  diffusion  distance  of  s  jump  steps.   For  each  s,  the  values  of{∆X(i, s)}Ni=1  are  covered  by  Ns
non-overlapping  intervals  of  lengthǫ(s).    The  interval  lengthǫ(s)  is  chosen  as  a  fraction  of  the  square  root  of  the
variance  of  the  ﬂuctuations {∆X(i, 1)},  which  is  independent  of  the  time  scale  s.   We  count  the  number  N j(s)  of  the
returns∆X(i, s) that fall  in the   jth  interval and the probability (or frequency) by means of

                                                                    p j(s)≡   N j(s)N−  s.

The Shannon entropy of the diffusion process is determined as

                                                                         Ns∑
                                                            I(s)=−           p j(s) ln p j(s).
                                                                         j=1

The scaling behavior is characterized by
                                                                     I(s)∼δ ln s,
whereδ  is the scaling exponent.
    For  fractional  Brownian  motions,  the  DEA  scaling  exponent  is  equal  to  the  Hurst  index,  that  is,δ=   H  [367],
which  does  not  necessary  hold  for  other  processes  such  as  L´evy  processes  [367,                  368].   For  ﬁnancial  assets,  it  seems
that  one  should  work  on  returns  to  obtain  approximatelyδ=   H;   otherwise,δ  will  be  greater  than  0.9  [369–                            371].
There  is  also  an  evident  ﬁnite-size  effect  that  the  entropy    I(s)  deviates  from  the  “theoretical”  value  for  small  scales
and  converges  to  the  theoretical  value  for  large  s  [368].    It  is  possible  to  overcome  this  drawback  using  the  idea  of
integrated moments [372]  (see Section              5.1.5  for details).
    A  natural generalization of  DEA  for  multifractal analysis is  to  replace the  Shannon entropy by  the  R´enyi  entropy
expressed in Eq. (16), leading to  the multifractal diffusion entropy analysis (MF-DEA), whose main output is a  spec-
trum of scaling exponentsδ(q) [373].  Analogous to the multiscale multifractal analysis based on the MF-DFA method
[326]  (see Section      2.5.4), the  multiscale  multifractal diffusion entropy analysis can be  designed [374].  The MF-DEA
method  suffers  some  drawbacks  [375].   We  notice  thatδ(q)  is  not  related  directly  to  the  well-known  generalized  di-
mensions  Dq.    Hence,  the  MF-DEA  method  unveils  multifractality  in  certain  mappings  of  the  original  time  series,

                                                                            35
but  not  in  original  time  series  per  se.   In  the  DEA  or  MF-DEA  method,  the  estimation  of  the  probabilities  p j(s)  has
a  crucial  impact  on  the  estimation  of  the  R´enyi  entropy  and  consequently  theδ(q)  spectrum  [376].    In  this  vein,  an
optimal choice ofǫ(s)  is required [376].

2.5.9.   Symbolization presentation and R´enyi dimensions
     Xu  and  Beck  applied  symbolic  dynamics  techniques  to  ﬁnancia  l  returns  to  determine  the  R´enyi  dimensions  or
the  generalized  dimensions  Dq  [377].   There  are  different  symbolization  techniques  that  can  convert  a  ﬁnancial  time
series{X(i)}Ni=1  into a symbol sequence.  Li and Wang proposed an approach by considering the speed of price changes,
which is quantiﬁed by an angle [              378,    379],

                                                             θi=  arctan[(X(i+ℓ)−  X(i))/ℓ],                                                                                                                       (151)

which  falls  within  the  interval  [−90◦, 90◦].   To  symbolize  the  raw  time  series,  we  partition  [−90◦, 90◦]  into  2n  subin-
tervals,   which   are   usually   almost   symmetric   with   respect  toθ =    0◦.     Li   and   Wang   considered   four   subintervals
(−90◦,−45◦), [−45◦, 0◦), [0◦, 45◦), and [45◦, 90◦)  and four corresponding symbols D,  d, r  and R, representing respec-
tively fast  falls,  slow falls,  slow rises and fast  rises of prices.
     The  symbolization method of  Xu  and  Beck  is  similar  [377].  They suggested  to  partition the  return domain∆Xi∈
(−1, 1)  into  2n  subintervals,  mapped  into  2n  symbols.   When  n=   1,  the  two  subintervals  are  (−1, 0)  and  [0,∞)  and
two  corresponding symbols d  and u,  representing respectively falling down and  rising of  the  prices.  When  n=  2,  the
four  subintervals  are  (−1,−c1),  [−c1, 0],  [0, c2)  and  [c2,∞],  where  c1∈   (0, 1)  and  c2∈  [0,∞]  are  parameters.   Note
that  Xu  and  Beck  used  open  intervals,  which  may  discard  some  data  points.  In  addition,  a  return  cannot  be  less  than
−1.  For stock markets with price limit  rules, the subintervals should be adjusted accordingly.
     For any manner of symbolization, ifθi  or∆Xi  is an element of a certain subinterval, the ith symbol S i  is determined
by  the  corresponding  symbol.    In  this  way,  the  raw  time  series  is  mapped  to  a  symbol  sequence{S i}.    The  symbol
sequence  is  divided  into  Nk   segments  of  equal  length  k.    For  any  given  k,  we  obtainω(k)=   (2n)k  allowed  symbol
subsequences or  conﬁgurations.  The  occurrence probabilit  y  of  each  conﬁguration    C j  of  length  k  can  be  determined,
denoted as  p j=  p(C=  C j).  Xu and Beck recalled the well-known R´enyi information [151]

                                                                                         ω(k)∑
                                                                      Iq=         1q− 1  ln   pqj                                                                                                                                           (152)
                                                                                          j=1

and the R´enyi dimension
                                                                      Iq              1       1        ω(k)∑
                                                       Dq=  limε→0   lnε=  limε→0    lnε   q− 1  ln         pqj,                                                                                                         (153)
                                                                                                       j=1
whereε=  (2n)−k.
     The   method  has   been   applied   to   the   daily   and   1-min  price   time   series   of   several  US   stocks   (Alcoa,   Bank   of
America,  General  Electric,  Intel,  Johnson &  Johnson, Coca  Cola  and  WalMart)  from January  1998 to  May  2013 and
the generalized dimensions were calculated and compared for different values of k  [377].


3.   Joint multifractal analysis

3.1.   Joint multifractal analysis based on partition functions (MF-X-PF)
     A  complex  system   usually  contains  two   or  more  distinct  multifractal  measures  that  are  distributed  simultane-
ously   on   the   same   geometric  support.     In   turbulent  ﬂows,   the   v  elocity,   temperature,   and   concentration  ﬁelds   are
embedded in  the  same  space  as joint  multifractal measures [266],  in  which the  scaling behavior of the  joint  moments
⟨[mx(s, i)]p/2[my(s, i)]q/2⟩of two multifractal measures mx  and my  over box size  s is investigated.  We can also call this
method  the  multifractal  cross-correlation analysis  based  on  the  partition  function  approach (MF-X-PF)  [380].   In  the
original framework, the orders are  p and q rather than  p/2 and q/2.  When considering the degenerate case of mx=  my
and  p=  q,  the  joint  moment  becomes⟨[mx(s, i)]q⟩,  which  recovers exactly  the  partition  function  approach presented


                                                                                  36
in  Section       2.1.   The  case  of  p=  q  has  been  investigated in  Ref.  [381].   There  are  also  mathematical  treatments  of  the
bivariate case [382,             383] and the general case  of many measures [384,                                    385].
      In natural science, the joint multifractal analysis has also been applied to study the joint multifractal nature between
topographic  indices  and  crop  yield  in  agronomy  [386,                                   387],   nitrogen  dioxide  and  ground-level  ozone  [388],   wind
patterns and land surface air  temperature [389], and temperature and nitrogen dioxide [390].

3.1.1.   General formalism  for arbitrary (p, q) pairs
      For  the  integrated  measures  mx(s, i)  and  mx(s, i)  in  the  ith  box  of  size  s,  the  local  singularity  strengthsαx  andαy
are deﬁned as [          380]
                                                                  mx(s, i)∼  sαx          and         my(s, i)∼  sαy.                                                                                                              (154)
Denoting Ns(αx,αy) as the number of boxes needed to cover the neighborhoods of points with singularitiesαx  andαy,
the fractal dimension of the set  is  determined according to

                                                                               Ns(αx,αy)∼  s− f (αx,αy),                                                                                                                                      (155)

in which   f (αx,αy) is the  joint distribution of the two singularities [266], or the joint multifractal spectrum.
      Through the joint partition function [380]
                                                                                     ∑
                                                              χxy(p, q, s)=                 [mx(s, i)]p/2 [my(s, i)]q/2,                                                                                                       (156)
                                                                                        t
we can obtain the  joint mass exponent functionτxy(p, q) as

                                                                                χxy(p, q, s)∼  sτxy(p,q).                                                                                                                                        (157)

Inserting Eq.  (154)  into  the  joint  partition function, rewriting the  sum  into  a  double integral overαx  andαy,  and  then
applying the steepest  descent approach to estimate the  integral at small  s values, we have

                                                                 τxy(p, q)=  pαx/2+ qαy/2−   f (αx,αy),                                                                                                            (158)

where
                                                    ∂ f (αx,αy)/∂αx=  p/2         and  ∂ f (αx,αy)/∂αy=  q/2.                                                                                   (159)
      Taking the  partial derivative of Eq. (158) over  p or q, we  have

                                                     ∂τxy(p, q)/∂p=αx/2         and  ∂τxy(p, q)/∂q=αy/2.                                                                                     (160)

We  can then obtain the double Legendre transforms:

                                                 αx(p, q)=  2∂τ(p, q)/∂p         and  αy(p, q)=  2∂τ(p, q)/∂q,                                                                         (161a)

and
                                                         f (αx,αy)=  pαx(p, q)/2+ qαy(p, q)/2−τxy(p, q).                                                                                      (161b)


      From the canonical perspective, we can obtain the   f (αx,αy) function directly by deﬁning two canonical measures


                                                              µxy(p, q, s, i)=       [mx(s, i)]p/2[my(s, i)]q/2∑
                                                                                           t[mx(s, i)]p/2[my(s, i)]q/2.                                                                                                     (162)
The two singularity strengthsαx(p) andαx(p) and the joint multifractal spectrum   fxy(p, q) can be  computed by linear
regressions in log-log scales using the following equations:
                                                                                        ∑
                                                               αx(p, q)=  lim              iµxy(p, q, s, i) ln mx(s, i)
                                                                                 s→0                      ln s       ,                                                                                                    (163a)
                                                                                               37
                                                                                                ∑
                                                                     αy(p, q)=  lim                tµxy(p, q, s, i) ln my(s, i)
                                                                                        s→0                         ln s       ,                                                                                                    (163b)
                                                                                          ∑
                                                                                             tµxy(p, q, s, i) ln[µxy(p, q, s, i)]
                                                               fxy(p, q)=  lims→0                                   ln s         .                                                                                        (163c)

 The joint mass exponent function can be obtained by using Eq. (158).

3.1.2.   Analytical results for binomial measures
      Xie  et  al.   derived  analytical  results  for  binomial  measures  with  multipliers  px  and  py  [380].   They  found  that  the
two integrated measures mx(s, i) and my(s, i) in boxes of size  s=  2l  are  related explicitly by

                                                             mx(s, i)=  C(s)[  my(s, i)]β=  e−γLsγ/ln 2[  my(s, i)]β,                                                                                           (164)

where 2L  is the length of the time  series,
                                                                                     β=   ln px− ln(1−  px)ln py− ln(1−  py),                                                                                                                                     (165)

and
                                                                               γ=β ln(1−  py)− ln(1−  px).                                                                                                                           (166)
When  px+  py=  1,  we  haveβ=−1.   When  px=   py,  we  haveβ=  1  and C(s)=  1.   When  both  px  and  py  are  greater
than  0.5  or  less  than  0.5,  that  is,  (px− 0.5)(py− 0.5)>  0,  we  haveβ>  0;  Otherwise,  when  (px− 0.5)(py− 0.5)<  0,
we haveβ<  0.
      The joint mass exponent function is expressed as [380]

                                                      τxy(p, q)=       pγ                                                         y+ (1−  py)Q]
                                                                           2 ln 2+τy(Q)=       pγ2 ln 2−  ln[pQ                         ln 2     .                                                                              (167)
where
                                                                                           Q=βp/2+ q/2                                                                                                                                               (168)
and
                                                                          τy(Q)=− ln[pQy+ (1−  py)Q]/ ln 2.                                                                                                                 (169)
It follows that
                                                               αx= γ                          pQy   ln py+ (1−  py)Q ln(1−  py)
                                                                         ln 2− βln 2                        pQy+ (1−  py)Q                                                                                                                       (170)
and
                                                                   αy=−    1            pQy   ln py+ (1−  py)Q ln(1−  py)
                                                                                ln 2                  pQy+ (1−  py)Q     .                                                                                                     (171)
We  obtain immediately the relationship betweenαx  andαy,

                                                                                           αx= γln 2+βαy,                                                                                                                                               (172)

which shows that  fxy(αx,αy) is a curve along this line rather than a surface and the line segment (172) is the projection
of   fxy(αx,αy) onto the  (αx,αy)  plane.







                                                                                                        38
      Xie  et al.  obtained that [380]
                                                                                       {                                    }
                                                             αy,min=    limQ→∞αy=  min                  −ln pyln 2,−ln(1−  py)ln 2
                                                                                                        {                                     }                                                                                       (173)
                                                             αy,max=     limQ→−∞αy=  max                   −ln pyln 2,−ln(1−  py)ln 2

such that the width of the singularity spectrum ofαy  is
                                                                                           ⏐⏐⏐ln(1−  py)− ln py⏐⏐⏐
                                                                              ∆αy=                      ln 2     .                                                                                                                               (174)

When   py =   0.5,∆αy =   0.     In  this  case,   the  measure  is  neither  multifractal  nor  monofractal  since  it  is  uniformly
distributed on the support.  Similarly, we have
                                                                                       {                                    }
                                                             αx,min=    lim                             −ln px
                                                                            Q→∞αx=  min                     ln 2,−ln(1−  px)ln 2
                                                                                                        {                                     }                                                                                      (175)
                                                             αx,max=     lim                               −ln px
                                                                            Q→−∞αx=  max                       ln 2,−ln(1−  px)ln 2

      Further, the multifractal spectrum   fxy(αx,αy)  can be expressed as [380]
                                                                           (1−py    )Q ln    (    py  )     [        (1−py)Q]           [        (1−py    )Q]
                                                                       Q       py              1−py      +    1+         py         ln    1+         py
                                         fxy(αx,αy)=      1ln 2                                                 (1−py    )Q             .                                                         (176)
                                                                                                         1+         py

It follows immediately that
                                                                 fxy(Q=  0)=  1          and           fxy(Q)=   fxy(−Q),                                                                                                     (177)
where   fxy(Q) ≜     fxy(αx,αy; Q).  It implies that   fxy(αx,αy) is  symmetric with respect to  the line  Q=  0.  In addition, it  is
found that
                                                                                       lim
                                                                                     Q→±∞  fxy(p, q)=  0.                                                                                                                                           (178)
When Q<  0, d fxy(Q)/dQ>  0 so that  fxy(Q) is a monotonically increasing function of Q.  When Q>  0, d fxy(Q)/dQ>
0 so that  fxy(Q) is a monotonically decreasing function of Q.  Therefore, the maximum of  fxy(Q) is 1 and its minimum
is 0.

3.1.3.   The case  p=  q
      The  multifractal  cross-correlation  analysis  based  on  statistical  moments  (MFSMXA)  proposed  in  Ref.  [381]  is
actually  a  special  case  of  MF-X-PF(p, q) when  p=  q.  We  call  it  MF-X-PF(q)  for consistence  [380].  In  this  case,  we
have
                                                                      τxy(q)=  q(αx+αy)/2−  fxy(αx,αy),                                                                                                                 (179)
where
                                                                ∂ fxy(αx,αy)/∂αx=∂ fxy(αx,αy)/∂αy=  q/2.                                                                                                    (180)
Taking the derivative of Eq. (179)  over q  and applying Eq. (180), we have

                                                                                     dτxy(q)
                                                                                         dq  =αx+αy2  .                                                                                                                                          (181)

Deﬁning that
                                                                                αxy ≜    [αx(q)+αy(q)]/2,                                                                                                                                  (182)

                                                                                                   39
we have
                                                                                αxy=  dτxy(q)/dq,                                                                                                                                         (183a)
and
                                                           fxy(αxy(q)) ≜     fxy(αx,αy)=  qαxy(q)−τxy(q),                                                                                               (183b)
which is the  Legendre transform.
      It  is derived that [380]
                                                                         τxy(q)=  [τx(q)+τy(q)]/2,                                                                                                                               (184)
and
                                                                          fxy(q)=  [ fx(q)+  fy(q)]/2.                                                                                                                                (185)
We  deﬁne the generalized joint Hurst exponent    Hxy(q) as

                                                                             τxy(q)=  qHxy(q)− 1.                                                                                                                                       (186)

It follows that
                                                                          Hxy(q)=   Hx(q)+  Hy(q)2    ,                                                                                                                                  (187)

where  Hx(q)  and  Hy(q)  are  the  generalized Hurst  exponent of  mx  and my.  These  relations were  observed numerically
using the MF-X-DFA method [141], the  MF-X-DMA method [105]  and the  MF-X-PF method with  p=  q [381].
      If we  can deﬁne the  canonical measures

                                                                 µxy(q, s, i)=       [mx(s, i)my(s, i)]q/2∑
                                                                                          t[mx(s, i)my(s, i)]q/2.                                                                                                                (188)
the two singularity strengthsαx(p) andαx(p) and the joint multifractal spectrum   fxy(p, q) can be computed directly:
                                                              ∑
                                         αxy(q)=  lim             tµxy(q, s, i) ln [mx(s, i)my(s, i)]1/2
                                                        s→0                             ln s             =αx(q)+αy(q)2    ,                                                             (189a)
where Eq. (163a) and Eq. (163b)  are used in the second equality, and
                                                                                      ∑
                                                                                         tµxy(q, s, i) ln[µxy(q, s, i)]
                                                          fxy(αxy(q))=  lim
                                                                               s→0                       ln s       .                                                                                            (189b)
The joint mass exponent function can be obtained by using Eq. (183b).
      Xiong and Shang proposed the weighted MF-X-PF(q) method (W-MFSMXA) [391].  Their numerical experiments
on two-exponent ARFIMA  processes,  binomial measures and NBVP  time  series  illustrate  that  W-MFSMXA  slightly
outperforms MF-X-PF(q) for relatively short time series.

3.2.   Joint structure  function analysis (MF-X-SF)
3.2.1.   General formalism  for arbitrary (p, q) pair
      The  joint  multifractal  analysis  based  on  the  structure  function  approach,  termed  MF-X-SF,  also  has  its  root  in
turbulence, in  which  the  joint  structure  function of  the  velocity  and  temperature ﬂuctuations was  introduced to  study
the  cross-correlation between  the  temperature and  velocity dissipation  ﬁelds  in  a  heated  turbulent  jet  [                                                  392,     393].  For
two self-similar time series  X(i) and Y(i)  with i=  1, 2,···, N,  the (p, q)-order joint structure function is  deﬁned as

                                                                                                                     N∑
                                 Kxy(p, q, s)=⣨∆X(i, s)p/2∆Y(i, s)q/2⟩=                1N−  s+ 1                         ∆X(i, s)p/2∆Y(i, s)q/2,                                                (190)
                                                                                                                    i=s
where∆X(i, s) =    X(i)−  X(i−   s)   and∆Y(i, s) =    Y(i)−  Y(i−  s).     The   special  case   of   p =    1   is   called   by   some
researchers  as   the   analogous  multifractal  cross-correlation  analysis   (AMF-XA)   [394],   in   which   the   authors   were
seemingly unaware of previous works [392,                              395].  Because the innovations∆X  and∆Y  could be negative, the structure
                                                                                            40
functions  Kxy(p, q, s)  are  usually  deﬁned  on  positive  integers  of     p  and  q.     Therefore,  it  is  more  convenient  to  use
absolute innovations to deﬁne the joint structure function

                                                                                                                          N∑
                               Kxy(p, q, s)=⣨|∆X(i, s)|p/2|∆Y(i, s)|q/2⟩=                1N−  s+ 1                             |∆X(i, s)|p/2|∆Y(i, s)|q/2,                                         (191)
                                                                                                                         i=s
which  is  widely  adopted  in  the  structure  function  approach,  as  well  as  in  the  multifractal  height  cross-correlation
analysis  (MFHXA)  [395].   If  there  is  a  scaling  relation  between  the  joint  structure  function  Kxy(p, q, s)  and  the  scale
s,  we can deﬁne the scaling exponent functionτxy(p, q) as

                                                                      Kxy(p, q, s)∼  sζxy(p,q)=  sτxy(p,q)+1,                                                                                                                   (192)

which is an  analog of the MF-X-PF(p, q) approach.
      We  further deﬁne  the  singularity functionsαx(p, q) andαy(p, q) as  the  partial  derivatives ofτxy(p, q) with  respect
to  p  and  q,  respectively,  as  in  Eq.  (161a).   The  singularity  spectrum  function   fxy(p, q) ≜      fxy(αx,αy)  can  be  obtained
as  in  Eq.  (161b).   We  note  that  it  is  hard  to  deﬁne  a  generalized  Hurst  index    Hxy(p, q)  in  a  consistent  way.   The  joint
structure function approach has been considered for the multifractal random walks [396].

3.2.2.   The case  p=  q
      A  special  case  of  the  MF-X-SF(p, q)  approach  when  p=   q  was  proposed  by  Kristoufek,  which  was  termed  the
multifractal height cross-correlation analysis [395].  For consistency, we call it the MF-X-SF(q) approach.  In this case,
the qth order joint structure function is deﬁned by

                                                                                                                        N∑
                                        Kxy(q, s)=⣨|∆X(i, s)∆Y(i, s)|q/2⟩=                1N−  s+ 1                          |∆X(i, s)∆Y(i, s)|q/2.                                                          (193)
                                                                                                                       i=s
If there is  a  scaling relation between the joint  structure function  Kxy(p, q, s) and the scale  s,  we  can deﬁne the  scaling
exponent functionτxy(q) as
                                                                          Kxy(q, s)∼  sτxy(q)+1=  sqHxy(q),                                                                                                                           (194)
where  Hxy(q)  are  the  generalized  bivariate  Hurst  exponents  or  the  generalized  joint  Hurst  exponents.   Under  certain
conditions, we  have [395]
                                                                              Hxy(q)=   Hx(q)+  Hy(q)2    .                                                                                                                                  (195)

      We  deﬁne the joint singularity function and its  spectrum thr  ough the Legendre transform:

                                                                                    αxy=  dτxy(q)/dq,                                                                                                                                          (196a)

and
                                                                                 fxy(αxy)=  qαxy−τxy.                                                                                                                                   (196b)
If Eq. (195) holds, we  have
                                                                                τxy(q)=τx(q)+τy(q)2                                                                                                                                                         (197)

according to the deﬁnitions thatτx(q)=  qHx(q)− 1,τy(q)=  qHy(q)− 1 andτxy(q)=  qHxy(q)− 1, and

                                                                               αxy(q)=αx(q)+αy(q)2                                                                                                                                                         (198)

according  to  the  deﬁnitions  that αx(q)=   dτx(q)/dq,αy(q)=   dτy(q)/dq  andαxy(q)=   dτxy(q)/dq.    It  follows  from
Eq. (196b) that
                                                                             fxy(αxy)=    fx(αx)+  fy(αy)2    .                                                                                                                              (199)


                                                                                                 41
3.3.   Multifractal wavelet  coherence analysis (MF-WCA)
3.3.1.   Cross  wavelet transformation and wavelet coherence analysis
      The  cross  wavelet  transform  (XWT)  of  two  time  series{Xi}Ni=1   and{Yi}Ni=1   was  introduced  by  Hudgins  et  al.    to
study the highly intermittent pattern in atmospheric turbulence [397], which is  deﬁned by

                                                                           Wxy(s, i)=Wx(s, i)Wy(s, i).                                                                                                                          (200)

The cross-wavelet power is
                                                                                  Pxy(s)=∥Wxy(s, i)∥.                                                                                                                                        (201)
The wavelet  coherenceρ2xy(s) is  deﬁned as
                                                                                                              ∑N
                                        ρ2xy(s)=    ∥Wxy(s, i)∥                                   (∑N            i=1|Wx(s, i)Wy(s, i)|
                                                        ∥Wx(s, i)∥∥Wy(s, i)∥=
                                                                                                       i=1 W2x(s, i))1/2(∑Ni=1 W2y (s, i))1/2.                                                         (202)

The  wavelet  coherence  is  able  to  uncover  the  co-movement  between  two  time  series  in  the  time-frequency  domain,
which is widely applied in  ﬁnance and economics [                                      398–    400].

3.3.2.   MF-X-WT
      Similar to the MF-X-PF method, Jiang et  al.  designed the multifractal cross wavelet analysis [401].  It generalizes
the concept of wavelet coherence and constructs the following joint partition function:

                                                                                           N∑
                                                                 χxy(p, q, s)=                 |Wx(s, i)|p/2|Wy(s, i)|q/2.                                                                                                         (203)
                                                                                          i=1
The deﬁnition of the partition function allows us to uncover   the more intricate relationship between the coherency and
the  scale  under  different  scopes,  which  corresponds to  a  cross-multifractal  behavior.   If  the  underlying  processes  are
jointly multifractal, the result  is a scaling behavior:

                                                                                  χxy(p, q, s)∼  sTxy(p,q),                                                                                                                                       (204)

whereTxy(p, q) is the joint mass exponent function, which can be estimated by regressing lnχxy(p, q, s) against ln s in
the scaling range for a  given pair (p, q).
      Analogous  to   the   double  Legendre  transform  in   the  joint  multifractal  analysis  based   on  the   partition  function
approach MF-X-PF(p, q) [380], we  deﬁne the joint singularity strength function    hx  and hy

                                                                          hx(p, q) =        2∂Txy(p, q)/∂p,                                                                                                                          (205)
                                                                          hy(p, q) =        2∂Txy(p, q)/∂q,                                                                                                                          (206)

and the multifractal spectrum  Dxy(hx, hy)

                                                                      Dxy(hx, hy)=  phx/2+ qhy/2−Txy.                                                                                                                 (207)

      When  p=  0  and  q=  0,  the  joint  partition  function in  Eq.  (203)  is  equal  to  the  number of  wavelet  coefﬁcients,  or
equivalently the total number of data points of the original series.  In other words, we have

                                                                                      χxy(0, 0, s)=  N,                                                                                                                                                (208)

which results in
                                                                                         Txy(0, 0)=  0.                                                                                                                                                    (209)
In contrast, in the MF-X-PF(p, q) method,τxy(0, 0)=−1.  Therefore,

                                                                                  Txy(p, q)/nequalτxy(p, q).                                                                                                                                        (210)
                                                                                                  42
It  follows  immediately  that  hx(p, q),  hy(p, q)  and  Dxy(hx, hy)  obtained from  the  MF-X-WT(p, q) method  are  different
from the joint  singularity strengthsαx(p, q),αy(p, q) and the  joint multifractal spectrum   fxy(αx,αy) obtained from the
MFXPF(p, q) method.
      Analogous to the partition function approach [157,                                158], we  can directly estimate  the joint singularity strength hx
and hy  and the joint multifractal spectrum  Dxy(p, q) using
                                                                                  1    ∑
                                                   hx(p, q) =        lims→0     ln s         µxy(p, q, s, i) ln|wx(s, i)|,                                                                                              (211)
                                                                                         i
                                                                                  1    ∑
                                                   hy(p, q) =        lims→0     ln s         µxy(p, q, s, i) ln|wy(s, i)|,                                                                                              (212)
                                                                                         i
                                                                                  1    ∑
                                                 Dxy(p, q) =        lim                      µxy(p, q, s, i) lnµxy(p, q, s, i).                                                                                (213)
                                                                          s→0   ln s     i

where
                                                               µxy(p, q, s, i)=|wx(s, i)|p/2|wy(s, i)|q/2χxy(p, q, s)   .

Thus we  can  directly determine the  joint  singularity strength functions hx(p, q) and  hy(p, q) and  the  joint  multifractal
function Dxy(p, q) from Eqs. (211–                      213)  through linear regressions.
      Extensive numerical experiments on two multifractal binomial measures unveil connections between the theoreti-
cal  joint multifractal formulas of  MF-X-PF(p, q) derived in  Ref.  [380]  and the  empirical joint multifractal features of
MF-X-WT(p, q) [401]:

                                                           Txy(p, q)+  p/2+ q/2− 1  = τxy(p, q),                                                                                                           (214)
                                                                                 hx(p, q)+ 1  = αx(p, q),                                                                                                            (215)
                                                                                 hy(p, q)+ 1  = αy(p, q),                                                                                                            (216)
                                                                            Dxy(hx, hy)+ 1  =         fxy(αx,αy),                                                                                                    (217)

whereτxy(p, q),αx(p, q),αy(p, q) and   fxy(αx,αy) are given in Eq. (167),  Eq. (170),  Eq. (171) and Eq. (176).
      Although  the  MF-X-WT(p, q)  method  is  able  to  successfully  identify  the  monofractal  or  multifractal  cross  cor-
relations  in  time  series,  the  results  show  non-negligible  deviations  from  the  theoretical  results  [401].    Nevertheless,
empirical applications to ﬁnancial returns and ﬁnancial vo    latilities uncover evident cross-multifractality [401].

3.3.3.   MF-X-WTMM
      The implementation of a joint multifractal analysis based on WTMM is not straightforward.  We can conveniently
determine  the   two   sets   of   modulus  maxima  linesLx   andLy   for   X(i)   and  Y(i).     However,   there  is   no  one-to-one
correspondence betweenLx  andLy  because  there  are  different  numbers of  modulus maxima  for  the  two  time  series
at  a  given  scale.   Lin  and  Sharif  found a  nice  solution  to  overcome this  difﬁculty  by  properly pairing  up  the  modulus
maxima  lines  inLx  andLy  [402,                      403].   Two  modulus  maxima  lines  inLx  andLy  are  paired  up  if  they  are  close  to
each other in the time  coordinate.  The joint partition function can thus be deﬁned as [                                                402]

                                                                               N j∑
                                                             Zp,q(s j)=             |Wx(i j,k, s j)|p|Wy(i j,k, s j)|q,                                                                                                         (218)
                                                                              k=1
which scales as a  power law with  respect to the scale  s:

                                                                                Zp,q(s)∼  sτxy(p,q)                                                                                                                                                (219)

Note that we use  p and q instead of  p/2 and q/2 following Ref. [402].  In this case, the Legendre transform is expressed
as [402]
                                                               αx=∂τ(p, q)/∂p, αy=∂τ(p, q)/∂q,                                                                                                         (220a)

                                                                                           43
and
                                                     f (αx,αy)=  pαx(p, q)+ qαy(p, q)−τ(p, q),                                                                                                 (220b)
where  p=∂ f/∂αx  and q=∂ f/∂αy.
     The  validity  of  the  MF-X-WTMM  method  is  conﬁrmed  by  numerica  l  experiments  on  coupled  random  binomial
cascades  [402].    Coupled  random  binomial  cascades  are  characterized  by  a  coupling  strength  parameter  g∈   [0, 1],
which  g=  0  and  g=  1  stand  respectively  for  uncoupled and  fully  coupled  time  series  [266].   Their  numerical  result
that the   f (αx,αy) spectrum collapses into a  one-dimensional curve in the  fully coupled case  is in  excellent agreement
with the analytical result of Eq. (172) [380].

3.3.4.   MF-X-WL
     Due  to  the  inherent  drawbacks  associated  with  the  multifractal  wavelet  transform  analysis,  the  MF-X-WT(p, q)
is  not  recommended for  multifractal  cross-correlation analysis.   In  addition  to  the  MF-X-WTMM  method,  a  suitable
alternative  candidate  is   the   wavelet  leaders  [256–              261].     Jiang   et  al.     generalized  the   MF-WL   method  to   the   joint
multifractal analysis based on wavelet leaders with two moment orders  p and q, termed MF-X-WL(p, q) [404].
     One  ﬁrst  obtains  the  wavelet  leaders ℓx( j, k)  andℓy( j, k)  of{Xi}N1    and{Yi}N1    at  different  scales   s=   2 j,  and  then
deﬁnes the joint moment with orders    p  and q  as [404]

                                                                                n j∑
                                                      Mxy(p, q,  j)=    1n j        ℓx( j, k)p/2ℓy( j, k)q/2,                                                                                                        (221)
                                                                               k=1

where n j  is the number of wavelet leaders at scale  s=  2 j.  When X=  Y  and  p=  q, we recover the traditional multifrac-
tal  formalism  based  on  wavelet  leaders  presented  in  Section                    2.3.3.   Further  scaling  analyses  follow  the  conventional
way as other similar  methods.
     Through  numerical  experiments,  it  is  found  that  the  MF-X-WL(p, q)  method  can  successfully  detect  joint  multi-
fractality  in  binomial  measures  and  monofractality  in  bivariate  fractional  Brownian  motions  [404].   The  method  has
also  been  used  to  investigate  the  joint  multifractality  between  the  daily  returns  of  the  DJIA  and  NASDAQ  indices,
and  between  their  daily  volatilities  as  well.   The  MF-X-WL(p, q)  method  works  for  both  positive  and  negative  (p, q)
values.  However, the method seemingly cannot provide very accurate results.

3.4.   Multifractal detrended cross-correlation analysis (MF-DCCA)
     Inspired  by  the  seminal  work  of  Podobnik  and  Stanley  on  the  detrended  cross-correlation  analysis  [140],  Zhou
proposed the multifractal detrended cross-correlation analysis, which is a combination of MF-DFA and DCCA [141].
The method was  initially termed MF-DXA  [141]  and then MF-X-DFA  so  that  one can distinguish it  from the  MF-X-
DMA method which is a combination of MF-DMA and DCCA  [105].
     Consider two time series{X(i)} and{Y(i)} of the same length N, where i=  1, 2,···, N.  Each time series is covered
with  Ns=  int[N/s] non-overlapping boxes of size  s.  The two segments of time series within the vth box [lv+ 1, lv+ s]
are  denoted  by  Xv(k)  and  Yv(k)  with  k=   1,···, s,   where  lv =   (v−  1)s.    Assume  that  the  local  trend  functions  of
{Xv(k)}sk=1  and{Yv(k)}sk=1  are{˜Xv(k)} and{˜Yv(k)},  respectively.  The cross-correlation for each box is calculated as

                                                                   s∑  [  Xv(k)−˜
                                                  F2v(s)=   1s                     Xv(k)][     Yv(k)−˜Yv(k)] .                                                                                               (222)
                                                                  k=1
The qth order cross-correlation is calculated as
                                                                               1m∑          1/q
                                                              F2xy(q, s)=       m        |Fv(s)|q                                                             (223)
                                                                                    v=1
when q/nequal0 and                                                                              .                                                                                                                 (224)
                                                                                   1   Ns∑
                                                          F2xy(0, s)=  exp        Ns        ln|Fv(s)|
                                                                                       v=1
                                                                                 44
We  then expect the following scaling relation

                                                                        Fxy(q, s)∼  sHxy(q).                                                                                                                                            (225)

     There  are  many  different  methods  for  the  determination  of˜Xv  and˜Yv.   The  local  trend  functions  could  be  poly-
nomials  [268,       405],  which  recovers  the  MF-X-DFA  method  [141].   The  local  trend  functions  could  also  be  moving
averages [275,        276],  in  which  case  the  algorithm is  called  MF-X-DMA  [105].  We  can  certainly use  other  detrending
approaches  as  described  in  Section               2.4.4.   When  X=  Y,  MF-X-DFA  reduces  to  MF-DFA  and  MF-X-DMA  reduces
to MF-DMA. The DCCA  method based on DMA was proposed by He  and Chen [406], which is a  special case  of the
MF-X-DMA method with q≡  2.
     For  dependent  multifractal  binomial  measure  pairs  [324]  and  dependent  multifractal  random  walk  pairs  [407],  it
is found numerically that [141]
                                                                   Hxy(q)=   Hx(q)+  Hy(q)2    ,                                                                                                                                  (226)

Further, He  and Chen gave an approximate derivation of the following inequality [408]:

                                                                   Hxy(q) ⩽     Hx(q)+  Hy(q)2    .                                                                                                                                 (227)

However, both relations (226) and (227)  deviate from many empirical results [141,                                        408–   410].
     For  the  monofractal  case  with  q=   2  such  as  two  coupled  autoregressive  fractional  moving  average  (ARFIMA)
signals with identical error terms, it  is shown with certain approximations that [140,                                     411,   412]

                                                                   Hxy(2)=   Hx(2)+ Hy(2)2    ,                                                                                                                                (228)

where  Hx(2)  and  Hy(2)  are  the  Hurst  exponents  of  time  series{Xi}  and{Yi}.    Kristoufek  derived  equation  (228)  for
two  ARFIMA  processes  regardless  of  the  correlation  between  error  terms,   as  long  as  it  remains  non-zero  and  for
an  ARFIMA  process  and  an  MA  process  [413].   Presenting  the  memory  effects  as  power  spectrum  in  the  frequency
domain,  Kristoufek  showed  that  the  bivariate  Hurst  exponent cannot  be  larger  than  the  average  of  the  separate  Hurst
exponents [414]:
                                                                   Hxy(2) ⩽     Hx(2)+  Hy(2)2    ,                                                                                                                                 (229)

which  holds  for  several  stochastic  processes  [413–                    420].    The  difference  can  be  qualiﬁed  by  the  DCCA  coefﬁcient
differentiation [421].
     There  are  also  variants  of  MF-X-DFA.  The  time-delay  MF-X-DFA  method  includes  a  time  delay  in  pairing  two
time series such that the detrended ﬂuctuation functions in    segments read [422]

                                                                  s∑ [  Xv(k)−˜
                                             F2v(δ; s)=   1s                       Xv(k)][     Yv(k+δ)−˜Yv(k+δ)].                                                                                (230)
                                                                k=1
Whenδ=   0,  we  recover  the  original  MF-X-DFA  method.   Shi  et  al.    generalized  the  multiscale  multifractal  analy-
sis  [326]  to  multiscale  multifractal  detrended  cross-correlation  analysis,  called  MSMF-DXA  [423].   This  method  is
applied  to  the  pairs  of  daily  returns  of  six  stock  market  indices  (DJIA,  NASDAQ,  S&P500,  SSCI,  SZCI  and  HSI)
[423],  return  pairs  (DJIA  vs  NYSE  and  DJIA  vs  HSI)  in  different  time  periods  [424],  and  pairs  of  opening  price,
closing price, highest price, lowest price and transaction volume of S&P 500, HSI, SSCI and ASX [425].  Cao and Xu
suggested  to  determine  the  local  trends  using  the  maximum  overlap  wavelet  transform  (MODWT)  [426].   Cao  et  al.
proposed  the  volatility-constrained multifractal  detrended  cross-correlation  analysis  [427].   Numerical  investigations
using mathematical models on the performance of these methods have not been conducted yet.
     The  MF-X-DFA  method  has  been  generalized  to  investigate  the  multifractal  cross-correlation  behavior  among
multiple  time  series,   which  leads  to  the  coupling  detrended  ﬂuctuation  analysis  (CDFA)  [                                  428].    For  multiple  time



                                                                                    45
series  X j(i) with   j=  1, 2,···, n, the local detrended ﬂuctuation function becomes

                                                                         s∑   n∏ [  X j,v(k)−˜
                                                        Fnv(s)=   1s                           X j,v(k)].                                                                                                               (231)
                                                                       k=1   j=1

Other  methods for  joint  multifractal  analysis  can  also  be  extended to  multiple  time  series  in  a  similar  way.  A  similar
treatment is to sum  the local ﬂuctuations of multiple time se  ries [429]:

                                                                        s∑   n∑  [  X j,v(k)−˜
                                                       F2v(s)=   1s                            X j,v(k)]   2.                                                                                                             (232)
                                                                       k=1  j=1

This method is reported to  work well  for two-component ARFIMA processes and binomial measures.
     The CDFA method has been applied to hourly concentrations of four air pollutants (NO2, NOx, total hydrocarbon
content  THC   and  O3)   at  Tehran   from  2   February  2006  to   31  July   2007  [428],   weekly   foreign  exchange  rates   of
three  currencies  (EUR/USD,  GBP/USD  and  JPY/USD)   from  14  December  1998  to  31  January  2011  [428],   daily
warehouse-out quantity  records  of  steel  products from  three  warehouses  (YZ,  JS  and  BY)  from  1  January  2009  to  1
October  2013  [430],  and  daily  closing  price  data  of  four  Asian  stock  markets’  indices  (SSEC,  NIKKEI  225,  KOSPI
and Indian SENSEX) from 1  July 2003 to 1 March 2015 [431].

3.5.   Multifractal cross-correlation analysis (MF-CCA)
     O´swie¸cimka   et   al.     argued  that   most   existing   methods  for   cross-correlation  analysis   often   present  the   serious
limitation  of  reporting spurious multifractal  cross-correlations when  there  are  none  [109].   To  remedy this  drawback,
they proposed the multifractal cross-correlation analysis (MF-CCA), which incorporates the sign of ﬂuctuations    Fv(s)
into the overall detrended ﬂuctuation    Fxy(q, s):
                                                          Ns∑           1/q
                                                                 1        sign (Fv(s))|Fv(s)|q            ,        q/nequal0
                                          Fxy(q, s)=            Ns   v=1                                                                                    (233)
                                                               Ns∑  sign (Fv(s)) ln|Fv(s)|
                                                                      ∑Ns
                                                              v=1        v=1 sign (Fv(s)) ,                            q=  0

where∑Nsv=1 sign (Fv(s))/nequalNs  in  many  cases.   When  Fxy(q, s)  is  negative  for  every  s,  one  represents−Fxy(q, s)  with
respect  to   s  in  the  log-log  plot  [109,       140].    The  MF-CCA  method  is  a  direct,  natural  multifractal  generalization  of
the  detrended  cross-correlation  analysis,  which  is  recovered  when  q=   2  [140].   Numerical  experiments  on  coupled
ARFIMA processes and coupled Markov-switching multifractal (MSM) processes validate the suitability of MF-CCA
in extracting joint monofractal or multifractal properties in  two time series.
     Combining the multifractal temporally weighted detrended ﬂuctuation analysis (MF-TWDFA) [                                            293] and the mul-
tifractal  cross-correlation  analysis  (MF-CCA)  [109],   Wei  et  al.    designed  the  multifractal  temporally  weighted  de-
trended  cross-correlation  analysis  (MFTWXDFA)  to  quantify  power-law  cross-correlations  in  two  time  series  and
conﬁrmed the validity of the MFTWXDFA method with numerical    experiments on bivariate fractional Brownian mo-
tions,  coupled  ARFIMA  processes,  and  multifractal  binomial  measures  [432].   The  method  is  applied  to  conﬁrm  the
presence  of  joint  multifractality  in  three  pairs  of  daily  stock  index  returns  (S&P  500  and  FTSE  100,  S&P  500  and
NIKKEI 225, and S&P  500 and HSI)  from 4 January 2001 to 30 September 2016 [432].
     The  idea  of  using  signed  ﬂuctuation applies to  other methods  ,  such  as  MF-X-DMA and  MF-X-SF.  Indeed, Wang
et al.  proposed a variant of the MF-X-SF(q) method by taking into account of the information on the innovation signs
in the deﬁnition of structure functions [              110]:

                                            K′xy(q, s)=⟨sign [∆X(i, s)∆Y(i, s)]|∆X(i, s)∆Y(i, s)|q⟩.                                                                                   (234)

This  deﬁnition  should  be  treated  with  caution  because    K′xy(q, s)  are  not  deﬁned  on  all    q∈ R  values  and  their  values
could  be  negative  so  that  the  scaling  relation  (194)  does  not  hold.    If  this  happens,  one  may  argue  that  there  is  no
cross-correlation scaling between the two time  series [110].
                                                                                46
3.6.   Multifractal detrended partial cross-correlation analysis (MF-DPXA)
     The observed long-range power-law cross-correlations between two time series may not be caused by their intrin-
sic relationship but by a common third driving force or by common external factors [433–                                                    435].  If the inﬂuence of the
common external  factors  on  the  two  time  series  are  additive,  we  can  use  partial  correlation  to  measure  their  intrinsic
relationship  [436].   To  extract  the  intrinsic  long-range power-law  cross-correlations between  two  time  series  affected
by  common  driving  forces,  Liu  developed the  detrended  partial  cross-correlation  analysis  (DPXA),  which  combines
the  ideas  of  DCCA  and  partial  correlation,  and  studied  the  DPXA  exponents  of  variable  cases  [437].    The  DPXA
method has been proposed independently by Yuan et  al.  [438].  Qian et  al.  generalized DPXA  to  analyze multifractal
time series,  resulting in the multifractal detrended partial cross-correlation analysis (MF-DPXA) [439].
     Assume that  two stationary time series{x(t)  :  t=  1,···, N}  and{y(t)  :  t=  1,···, N},  such as returns, depend on n
common external driving factors characterized by a  sequence of  time  series{zi(t)  :  t=  1, 2,···, N}  with  i=  1,···, n.
Each  time  series  is  partitioned  into  Ns=  int[N/s]  non-overlapping windows  of  size  s.   For  each  window,  say  the  vth
window [lv+ 1, lv+  s]  where lv=  (v− 1)s, we calibrate respectively two  linear regression models for xv  and yv,
                                                                          {xv=  Zvβx,v+ rx,v

                                                                              yv=  Zvβy,v+ ry,v ,                                                                                                                                     (235)

where xv=  [xlv+1,..., xlv+s]T, yv=  [ylv+1,..., ylv+s]T, rx,v  and ry,v  are the vectors of the residuals, and
                                                               zTv,1...=z1(lv+ 1) ···         zn(lv+ 1)                                                                                             (236)
                                                      Zv=                                 ...    ...    ···

                                                                  zTv,p            z1(lv+  s)  ···         zn(lv+  s)

is  the  matrix  of  the  n  external  forces  in  the  vth  window,  where  xT  is  the  transform  of  x.   We  obtain  the  estimates   ˆβx,v
and  ˆβy,v  of the  n-dimensional parameter vectorsβx,v  andβy,v  and the sequence of residuals
                                                                          {rx,v=  xv− Zv ˆβx,v

                                                                              ry,v=  yv− Zv ˆβy,v .                                                                                                                                     (237)

We  further obtain the residual proﬁles, that  is,
                                                                    {Rx,v(k)=∑kj=1 rx(lv+  j)

                                                                         Ry,v(k)=∑kj=1 ry(lv+  j) ,                                                                                                                           (238)

where k=  1,···, s.
     Assuming  that˜Rx,v  and˜Ry,v  are  respectively the  local  trend  functions of  Rx,v  and  Rx,v,  the  detrended partial  cross-
correlation in each window can be computed as

                                                                      s∑  [  Rx,v(k)−˜
                                                    F2v(s)=   1s                          Rx,v(k)][     Ry,v(k)−˜Ry,v(k)],                                                                                        (239)
                                                                     k=1
and the qth order detrended partial cross-correlation is
                                                                                        Ns∑                1/q
                                                              Fxy:z(q, s)=              1            |F2v(s)|q/2                                                               (240)
                                                                                    Ns− 1       v=1

when q/nequal0 and                                                                                                                                                                                                             (241)
                                                                                             1     Ns∑
                                                                Fxy:z(0, s)=  exp           Ns         ln|Fv(s)|
                                                                                                  v=1



                                                                                         47
when q=  0.  We  then expect the scaling relation

                                                                          Fxy:z(q, s)∼  sHxy:z(q).                                                                                                                                         (242)

     According to the standard multifractal formalism, the multifractal mass exponentsτ(q) can be used to characterize
the multifractal properties as
                                                                      τxy:z(q)=  qHxy:z(q)−  D f,                                                                                                                                 (243)
where D f=  1 is the fractal dimension of the geometric support of the time series [139].  Ifτ(q) is a nonlinear function
of q, the  partial cross-correlations of the time  series are multifractal.  According to  the Legendre transform, we obtain
the singularity strength functionα(q) and the multifractal spectrum   f (α)  as [132]
                                                                  {αxy:z(q)=  dτxy:z(q)/dq

                                                                       fxy:z(q)=  qαxy:z−τxy:z(q) .                                                                                                                        (244)

     The  MF-DPXA  method  is  an  extension  to  MF-DCCA  methods.    Different  choices  of  the  local  trends  result  in
different  variants.   When  MF-DPXA  is  implemented  with  DFA  or  DMA,  we  obtain  MF-PX-DFA  or  MF-PX-DMA.
Using two  binomial measures contaminated by  Gaussian  noise with  very low  signal-to-noise ratio,  numerical experi-
ments show  that  MF-DPXA  is  signiﬁcantly  advantageous over M  F-DCCA  [439].  It  is  also  easy  to  extend the  DPXA
idea  to  other  methods  of  joint  multifractal  analysis.    In  this  vein,  the  performance  of  different  MF-DPXA  methods
should be investigated and compared.


4.   Multifractal models

4.1.   Multiplicative cascade models
4.1.1.   Deterministic  multinomial models
     Multiplicative  cascade  models  generate  multinomial  measures  that  are  constructed  in  a  recursive  manner  [123].
Multiplicative  cascades  exist  in  ﬁnancial  markets  since  th  ere  is  evidence  of  a  causal  information  cascade  from  large
scales  to  ﬁne  scales  [      440,    441].   The  multiplicative  cascade  process  starts  with  an  interval  [0, 1]  on  with  the  measure
is  uniformly  distributed.    Without  loss  of  generality,  assume  that  the  total  measure  on  the  interval  is  1.    At  the  ﬁrst
iteration, the  interval  is  divided into  b  segments whose  lengths are{si  :  i=  1,···, b}  and  the  measure  is  redistributed
on each segment such that the  measure on the  ith segment is mi.  At  the second iteration, each segment is divided into
b  smaller  segments  and  the  measure  on  it  is  redistributed  in  the  same  way.   This  procedure  goes  to  inﬁnity,  resulting
in a  multiscale multinomial measure.  The mass exponent function can be  solved from the following equation [132]:
