4  January  1988  to  16  July  2001  using  MF-WT  [732]),  and  the  Spanish  IBEX  35  from  1990  to  2000  using  MF-WT
[733].
    Although there  is  overwhelming empirical  evidence indicating  the  presence  of  multifractality  in  the  daily  returns
of  stock  market  indices,  an  exception  was  reported  on  the  daily  Bombay  stock  exchange  index  (BSE)  data  of  year
2000,  in  whichζ(q)  is  found  to  be  linear  against  q  [734].    This  is  most  likely  due  to  the  fact  that  the  sample  size
(245  data  points)  is  too  short  so  that  it  is  difﬁcult  to  determ  ine  the  scaling  range.   In  addition,  there  are  studies  using
prices  as  the  variable∆X  in  the  MF-DFA  analysis  such  that  the  detrending is  conducted on  the  “cumulative  sum”  of
prices  [463,   734–  736],  which  either  results  in  incorrect  estimates  ofζ(q)  or  H(q)  or  misinterpretations of  the  results
on  reshufﬂing.  Indeed,  the  measure  derived from  the  time  ser  ies  under study  should  be  stationary  in  order  to  qualify
as  a  genuine  measure.     Prices  and  log-prices  are  not  stationary  and  using  them  is  well-known  to  lead  to  spurious
conclusions.  In regression analysis, this is  known as the spurious regression problem [737,                        738].

6.1.2.   High-frequency stock market  indices
    High-frequency  indices  have  been  studied  for  different  stock  markets,  such  as  the  US  market  (15-min  S&P  500
index  from  June  24  to  September  30  in  1999  using  MF-SF  [739],   5-min  S&P500  index  from  1  February  2001  to
1  February  2002  using  WTMM  [740],  and  5-min  DJIA  from  19  September  1994  to  16  October  2002  using  MF-SF
[741]), the French market (30s CAC 40 from 3 January 1993 until 31 December 1996 using MF-SF [742]), the German
market  (1-min  DAX  data  from  28  November  1997  to  30  December  1999  using  MF-SF  [724]),  the  Russian  market
(30-min RTS-70 from 9 December 1996 to 22 February 2002 using MF-WT [732]), the Korean market (1-min KOSPI
from 30 March  1992 to  30 November 1999 using MF-SF  [743–                       747]),  the  Polish  market (1-min WIG20 from  1  April
1999 to  31  October  2005  using  MF-DFA  [748]),  the  Chinese  market  (1-min  SSEC  from  4  May  1998  to  1  June  2005
using MF-DFA [749], 5-min SSEC from January 2003 to April 2008 using MF-DMA [290], and 5-min CSI 300 from
4  April  2005  to  19  January  2012  using  MF-DMA  [612]),  the  Taiwan  market  (1-min  TAIEX  from  February  2000  to
December 2003 using MF-SF and MF-DFA [728] and from 2 January 1999 to 28 August 2007 using MF-DFA [750]),
the  Spanish  market  (13s  IBEX  35  from  January  2nd  2009  to  December  31st  2010  using  MF-DFA  [751]),  the  1-min
Euro  Stoxx  50  index  from  May  2008  to  April  2009  using  MF-DFA  [585],  and  the  daily  CDS  of  11  sector  indices  in
the US  stock market from 14 December 2007 to 31 December 2014 using MF-DFA  [752].

6.1.3.   Stock market sector indices
    Some  studies  considered  stock  market  sector  indices.     Sector  indices,   as  well  as  stock  market  indices,   can  be
viewed as portfolios of stocks.  Using the MD-DFA method, Wang, Xiang and Pandey investigated the daily computer
sector  index  and  electronics  sector  index  of  China’s  growth  enterprise  market  from  September  2010  to  August  2011
[753],  Kim  et  al.    analyzed  the  daily  prices  of  144  equity  funds  from  1  January  2002  to  31  December  2010  using
MF-DFA  [754],  Zhuang,  Wei  and  Ma  worked  on  10  sector  indices  of  China  stock  market  from  1  January  2005  to  3
                                                                         80
April  2013  [755],  Yang,  Zhu  and  Wang  studied  the  daily  CSI  energy  sub-industry  index  from  4  January  2005  to  15
June  2015  [756],  and  Shahzad  et  al.   researched  11  sector  indices  in  the  US  stock  market  from  14  December  2007 to
31 December 2014 [752].

6.1.4.   Individual stocks
    The  US  stock  market  contributes  the  largest  number  of  individual  stocks  that  have  been  studied,  containing  5-
min  prices  of  a  particular  stock  traded  on  the  New  York  Stock  Exchange  (NYSE)  from  4  January  1993  to  30  June

Yahoo!,  Microsoft  and  IBM  from  1  February  2001  to  1  February  2002  using  WTMM  [740],  daily  prices  of  stock
Intel  between  January  1990  and  December  2002  using  MF-SF  and  MF-DFA  [194],  1-min  prices  of  stock  Johnson
and Johnson during the  year of  2000 using MF-SF  and  MF-DFA [194],  5-min prices of  stock Intel  during the  year of
2000 using MF-SF  and MF-DFA [194], tick-by-tick prices of 30 DJIA stocks from 1 Dec  1997 to  31 Dec 1999 using
MF-DFA  and  WTMM  [681],  daily  prices  of  30  DJIA  stocks  using  MF-SF  [457],  high-frequency prices  of  30  DJIA
stocks  from  the  1  July  to  31  December  2004  using  MF-DFA  [758],  trade-by-trade  returns  of  stock  GE  for  calendar
years 2000-2003 [111], 1-min to  10-min prices of 31 DJIA  stocks for the years 2008-2011 using MF-DFA [759].
    Studied  time  series  of  individual  stocks  in  other  markets  include  tick-by-tick  data  of  30  DAX  companies  from  1
Dec  1997 to  31  Dec  1999 using  MF-DFA  and  WTMM  [453,                     681],  daily  prices of  202  constituent  stocks  of  NIKKEI

6.1.5.   Equity futures
    The multifractal nature of equity futures has been conﬁrmed   on 10-sec prices of the DAX futures during 60 trading
days  using  WTMM  [711],  5-min  prices  of  the  CSI  300  futures  from  16  April  2010  to  12  April  2012  using  MF-DFA
[765],  10-min prices of  the  CSI  300 futures IF1009 from 16  April 2010 to  17  September 2010 using MF-DFA  [766],
and 5-min prices of the CSI  300 futures from 30 June 2011 to 7 June 2013 using A-DFA [358].

6.1.6.   Foreign exchange rates
    The global foreign exchange market is the largest ﬁnancial m  arket by transaction volumes.  The multifractal nature
of foreign exchange rates has been conﬁrmed by numerous empi  rical studies.  Some excellent works researched dozens
of  foreign  exchange  rates.   For  instance,  Vandewalle  and  Ausloos  [767]  and  Di  Matteo  et  al.   [138,                 176]  investigated
respectively 23 and  29 daily foreign exchange rates using  the MF-SF  method.  Dro˙zd˙z et  al.  performed an  interesting
study  using  MF-DFA  on  the  1-min  rates  from  21:00  on  2  January  2004  to  21:00  on  30  March  2008  of  two  foreign
exchange  triangles  (USD/EUR,  EUR/GBP  and  GBP/USD)  and  (JPY/GBP,  GBP/CHF  and  CHF/JPY)  [622].   Pont  et
al.  obtained the ensemble singularity spectrum of the daily returns of 9 currency exchange rates (pairs of AUD, USD,
EUR, JPY  and CHF  except for AUD/CHF)  from 1992 to 1997 using MF-WT [733].
    The  Japanese  Yen  (JPY)  with  respect  to  the  USD  is  among  the  most  studied.    In  ﬁnance,  this  currency  pair  is
particularly  interesting  due  to  its  enormous  transaction  volume  and  for  the  carry  trade  that  have  been  implemented
for  decades  as  a  major  funding  source.   In  the  standard  logic,  one  borrows in  Yens  at  the  low  interest  rates  that  have
been  prevalent  in  Japan  for  decades,  then  sells  the  Yens  to  buy  U.S.  dollars,  which  are  invested  in  higher  yielding
instruments such as U.S. treasury bonds.  This carry trade, which contributes to pushing the Yen value lower, has been
part  of  the  strategy  of  the  Japanese  society,  which  is  so  dependent on  exports for  its  economic health  and  on  the  safe
haven status of its currency.  Time series are recorded at different frequencies, such as daily data ( from 1 June 1973 to
1  June  1988 using  MF-SF  [173],  from 1  January  1980 to  31  December 1996 using  MF-SF  [690],  from January 1971
to  June  2003  using  MF-SF  [768],  from  1  January  1990  to  2  November 2003  using  MF-SF  [769],  from  January  1971
to  June  2003  using  MF-SF  [725],  from  1  January  1990  to  2  November 2003  using  MF-SF  [770],  from  15  December
                                                                          81
1998  to  9  November 2008  using  WTMM  [771],  from  2  January  1975  to  12  November 2010  using  MF-DMA  [611],
and  from  1991  to  2005  using  MF-DFA  [772]),  tick-by-tick  data  (from  1  March  1986  to  1  March  1989  using  MF-
SF  [173]),  1-min  data  (in  2005  [773]  and  2008  [774]  using  MF-DFA),  and  5-min  data  (from  1  February  2001  to  1
February 2002 using WTMM [740]).
    For the Deutsche Mark (DEM) with respect to the USD,  the MF-SF  method has been applied to  daily data during
different periods (  from  1  June  1973  to  1  June  1988 [173],  from  1  January  1980  to  31  December  1996  [690],  from  1
January 1990 to  2 November 2003 [770],  and from 4  June 1973 to  31 December 2001 [769]), tick-by-tick data (from
1  March  1986  to  1  March  1989  [173]  and  from  1  October  1992  until  30  September  1993  [775]),  15s  data  (from  1
October  1992  until  30  September  1993  [742]),  and  5-min  data  (from  4  January  1987  to  31  December  1998  [776]).
The MF-DFA  method has also been applied to the  20s FX  rates from 1  October 1992 to  30 September 1993 [777].
    For the  Euro exchange rates,  studies have been carried out for 1-min EUR/USD  in  2005 using MF-DFA [773],  5-
min EUR/USD from 5 January 2006 to 31 December 31 2007 using MF-SF [461], daily EUR/USD from 15 December
1998 to 9 November 2008 using WTMM [771] daily EUR/USD from 1 January 1990 to 2 November 2003 using MF-

January 1999 to 12 November 2010 using MF-DMA [611], 1-min EUR/USD in 2008 using MF-DFA [774], and daily
data  of  Croatian  Kuna  (HRK),  Czech  Koruna  (CZK),  Hungarian  Forint  (HUF),  Polish  Zlot  (PLN),  Romanian  Leu
(RON), Slovak Koruna (SKK) and Slovenian Tolar (SIT) against UER from 4 January 1999 to  31 August 2010 using
MF-DFA [297],
    For  the  UK  GBP  exchange  rates,  multifractal  analyses  have  been  performed  on  tick-by-tick  USD/GBP  from  1
March  1986  to  1  March  1989  using  MF-SF  [173],  daily  USD/GBP  from  1  June  1973  to  1  June  1988  using  MF-SF
[173], daily GBP/USD from 1 January 1990 to 2 November 2003 using MF-SF [770], daily GBP/USD from 2 January
1975 to 12 November 2010 using MF-DMA [611], and daily GBP/USD from 15 December 1998 to 9 November 2008
using WTMM  [771].
    For  the  Chinese  Yuan,  multifractal  investigations have  been  conducted on  daily  CNY/USD  from  25  July  2005 to
12  November  2010  using  MF-DMA  [611],  daily  CNY/USD  from  19  November  1997  to  April  2008  and  CNY/EUR

and CNY/HKD from 13 January 1994 to 2 August 2012 using MF-DFA [780], daily prices of the onshore and offshore
RMB  exchange  rates  (CNY  and  CNH)  from  2  May  2012  to  27  January  2016  using  MF-DFA  [730],  and  daily  CIB-

    For  the  Korean  Won  (KRW)  exchange rates,  the  studied  time  series  include daily  KRW/USD  from April  1981 to

    For the Australian Dollar (AUD), investigations have been conducted on 1-min AUD/USD in 2005 using MF-DFA

    The  multifractal  nature  in  quite  a  few  other  currencies  in  different  economies  has  also  been  researched,  such  as

6.1.7.   Commodities
    In  the  commodity  markets,  the  multifractal  properties  of  gold  prices  and  of  returns  have  been  studied,  such  as
tick-by-tick  prices  of  gold  (XAU/USD)  from  1  March  1986  to  1  March  1989  using  MF-SF  [173],  daily  gold  prices
from  February  1991  to  May  1997  using  MF-SF  [691],  daily  prices  of  gold  traded  in  COMEX  over  the  period  of  13
July  1990  to  15  September  2009  using  MF-DFA  [786],  daily  LME  gold  prices  from  1968  to  2010  using  MF-DFA
[787],  daily  gold  prices  from  January  1973  to  October  2011  using  MF-DFA  [788],  daily  AU99.99  closing  prices  of
the  Shanghai  Gold  Exchange from  30  October  2002  and  30  November 2012  using  MF-DFA  [789],  daily  gold  prices
in  the  Indian  market  and  daily  global  consumer  price  index  from  January  1985  to  June  2013  using  MF-DFA  [790],
daily gold consumer price index (CPI)  and  the  gold market in  China, India  and Turkey from 1993 to  July  2013 using
MF-DFA [791], and daily gold prices of China and India from 1985 to 2013 using MF-DFA [621].
    In the oil and gas market, studies have been conducted on daily spot prices of WTI crude oil (from November 1981
to about 2001 using MF-SF [792], from 2 January 1990 to 28 February 2001 using MF-SF [793], from 2 January 1986
to  28  May  2004  using  MF-SF  [794],  from  20  May  1987  to  30  September  2008  using  MF-DFA  [795],  from  1986  to
2008  using  MF-SF  [796],  from  20  May  1987  to  14  October  2009  using  MF-DFA  [797],  from  2  January  1991  to  31
December  2013  [798],   from  2  January  1986  to  28  September  2012  using  MF-DFA  [799],),  Brent  crude  oil  (from
November 1981  to  about  2001  using  MF-SF  [792],  from  20  May  1987  to  30  September  2008  using  MF-DFA  [795],
from  20  May  1987  to  14  October  2009  using  MF-DFA  [797],  Dubai  crude  oil  from  November  1981  to  about  2001
using  MF-SF  [792],   conventional  gasoline  (New  York  Harbor)  from  2  January  1991  to  31  December  2013  [798],
heating  oil  (New  York  Harbor)  from  2  January  1991  to  31  December  2013  [798],  jet  fuel  (US  Gulf  Coast)  from  2
January  1991  to  31  December  2013  [798],  and  natural  gas  (Henry  Hub,  LA)  from  24  January  1991  to  28  February
2001 [793].  Other related studies include daily prices of the WTI  crude oil futures traded in  NYMEX from 2 January
1985 to  10  May  2011 using  MF-DMA  [107],  daily  data  of  DJ  Brent  crude  index from January 1999 to  January 2015
and  DJ  energy  index  and  DJ  petroleum  index  from  January  2006  to  January  2015  using  MF-DFA  [800],  daily  crack
spread  of  gasoline  which  is  the  difference  between  the  spot  prices  of  gasoline  (New  York  Harbor)  and  WTI  crude
oil  from  2  January  1986  to  26  July  2011  using  MF-DMA  [801],  monthly  price  indexes  of  World  Bank  non-fuel  and
World  Bank  energy  from  1960  until  2013  using  MF-DFA  [800],  and  a  steam  coal  FOB  price  in  Qinhuangdao  Port
from 2006 to 2013 [802].
    For  the  electricity  market,  researchers  have  studied  hourly  spot  prices  in  the  Spain  electricity  exchange  from  1
January 1998 to 31 May 2006 using MF-DFA [803], 30-min electricity prices and demand data in Australia’s  national
electricity market from December 1998 to June 2008 using MF-DFA [804], 1-hour electricity prices and demand data
from  1  January  2000  to  20  June  2008  for  Alberta  and  from  1  May  2002  to  30  June  2008  for  Ontario  in  Canadian
electricity  markets  using  MF-DFA  [805],  electricity  prices  of  California  1999-2000 and  PJM  2001-2002  using  MF-
DFA [806], 15-min day-ahead monthly bid prices of 5 bid areas from 1 April 2012 to 31 March 2014 using MF-DFA
[807],  and  daily  electric  load  records  of  a  certain  power  system  from  1  January  1999  to  31  December  2011  using
MF-DFA [808].
    Concerning  multifractal  detrended  ﬂuctuation  analysis  of    agricultural  commodities,  Matia  et  al.    considered  the
daily  prices  of  9  commodities  over  10-30  years  [185],  He  and  Chen  studied  the  daily  closing  prices  of  hard  winter
wheat futures on the Zhengzhou Commodity Exchange from 28 December 1993 to 18 September 2009, and soy meal
                                                                         83
futures from  17  July  2000  to  18  September 2009,  No.  1  soybean  futures from  15  March  2002 to  18  September 2009
and corn futures from 22 September 2004 to 18 September 2009 on the Dalian Commodity Exchange [809], Kim et al.
researched the  daily  prices of  leek,  radish, onion, and Korean cabbage from 3  January 2001 to  23 September 2009 in
the  public  wholesale  market  of  Seoul  Agricultural  &  Marine  Products  Corporation [810],  Wang  and  Hu  investigated
the daily prices of four agricultural commodity futures (corn, soybean, wheat and rice) on the Chicago Board of Trade
(CBOT) from 1 January 2000 to 31 September 2014 [811], and Delbianco et al.  worked on the monthly data of World
Bank’s soybean price index and agricultural price index from 1968 until 2013 [800].

6.1.8.   Interest rates
    Multifractal  analyses  on  interest  rates  and  their  futures  have  mainly  used  the  MF-SF  method  and  have  been  con-
ducted on  daily  data  of  daily  Korean treasury  bond futures from December 2001 to  June  2002 for KTB206  and  from
March  2002  to  September  2002  for  KTB209  [812],  US  treasury  rates  with  different  maturities  from  1990  to  2001
[176],  daily  Eurodollar  interbank  interest  rates  with  different  maturity  dates  from  1990  to  1996  [176],  daily  Pound
sterling, US dollar, Japanese Yen, Australian dollar and the EURO, LIBOR and Indonesian Rupiah for different matu-
rities (3, 6, 9 and 12 months) from January 2000 to December 2005 using MF-SF and MF-DFA [813], daily Eurodollar
rates from 1990 to 1996 [138],  and US  treasury bond rates from 1997 to  2001 [138].
    The  MF-DFA  method  has  also  been  applied  to  the   interest  rates  of  daily  Pound  sterling,   US  dollar,   Japanese
Yen,  Australian  dollar,  the  EURO,  LIBOR  and  Indonesian  Rupiah  for  different  maturities  (3,  6,  9  and  12  months)
from  January  2000  to  December  2005  [813],  daily  US  treasury  bonds  from  2  January  1970  to  30  November  2006
[814], 1-min Korean Treasury Bond (KTB412) futures from 1 July  2004 to  31 December 2004 [814],  daily Shanghai
interbank offered rate  (SHIBOR)  with  eight  term  values from 8  October 2006 to  31  December 2012 [815],  and  daily
US Effective Federal Funds from 1 January 2000 to 31 September 2014 [811].

6.1.9.   Illusionary multifractality
    Some studies applied the MF-PF method to stock market indices or asset prices such that the ﬁnest measure deﬁned
at  each  time  t  is  the  ratio  of  the  index  price  at  t  to  the  sum  of  all  index  prices.   Representative  works  are  obtain  from
the  Hong  Kong  market  (1-min  HSI  from  3  January  1994  to  28  May  1997  [816,                    817]  and  30  continuous trading  days
from  3  January  1994  [818]),  the  Shanghai  stock  market  (1-min  SSEC  from  19  January  1999  to  30  December  2005
[819],  5-min  SSEC  from  January  1999  to  July  2001  [820],  from  4  May  1998  to  1  June  2005  [749],  from  4  January
2005  to  30  December  2005  [821],  and  from  2  January  2004  to  29  June  2012  [822],  and  daily  SSEC  from  4  January
2005 to  30  December 2005  [821]),  the  Taiwan  stock  market  (daily  TSPI  from 1987  to  2002 [823]  and  1-min  TAIEX
from 3 May 1999 to 30 November 2007 [824]), the US market (5-min S&P 500 from 2 January 2004 to 29 June 2012
[822]), and the oil  price from 1973 to 2007 [825].
    All  these  works  produce  illusionary  multifractality  simply  because  these  indices  are  not  singular  and  a  charac-
teristic  is  that  the  singularity  is  close  to  1  such  that  the  singularity  width∆α  is  close  to  0  [826,         827].   However,  this
illusionary  multifractality  seems  able  to  capture  to  some  extent  the  ﬂuctuation  characteristics  of  indices  and  has  the
potential power of volatility forecasting (see Section                8.3).
    An exception is reported for the hourly day-ahead and real-time electricity prices in the Pennsylvania-New Jersey-
Maryland  (PJM)  electricity  market  from  1  January  2008  to  31  December  2012,   in  which  the  singularity  width  is
signiﬁcantly larger than 0  [      828].  This is  due to the  fact  that the  electricity prices ﬂuctuat  e widely and the  two  investi-
gated price time  series looks like  stock volatility time series (see Fig.  2 of Ref. [828]).

6.2.   Volatilities
6.2.1.   Stock markets
    For  the  US  stock  market,  multifractality  has  been  reported  to  be  present  in  1-min  S&P  500  index  volatility  from
1  January  1982 to  31  December  1999  using  MF-PF  [159]  and  the  multiplier  method  [312],  high-frequency volatility
of  the  30  DJIA  stocks  from  1  July  2004 to  31  December 2004 using  MF-DFA  [758],  1-min volatility  of  the  31  DJIA
stocks  from  2008  to  2011  using  MF-CCA  [759],   daily  DJIA  volatility  from  18  May  1995  to  18  May  2015  using
MF-PF [829],  and daily NASDAQ  from 18 May 1995 to 18 May 2015 using MF-PF  [829].
    For  the  Chinese  stock  market,  researchers  studied  1-min  volatility  of  1139  Chinese  stocks  and  2  indexes  (SSCI
and  SZCI)  from  January  2004  to  June  2006  using  MF-PF  [165],  daily  volatility  of  36  SSE  180  stocks  from  9  July

                                                                           84
2002 to  9  July  2007 using MF-SF  [830],  1-min SSEC  volatility  from 20  September 2007 to  10  May  2010 using MF-
DFA  [831],  5-min SSEC  and SZCI  volatility  from 4  January 2002 to  31  December 2008 using  MF-DFA  [832],  daily
volatilities of the CSI energy index from 4 January 2005 to 15 June 2015 using MF-DFA [756], daily SSEC and SZCI
volatility  from  18  May  1995  to  18  May  2015  using  MF-PF  [829],  and  5-min  realized  volatility  of  the  SSEC  index
from 1 January 2000 to 31 May 2014 and the S&P  500 index from 2 January 1996 to 24 June 2013[833].
    Other  studies  considered  daily  DAX  volatility  from  1  October  1959  to  30  December  1998  using  MF-PF  [723],
1-min upward and  downward volatility  (or positive  and  negative returns) of  the  DAX  index  from 28  November 1997
to  30  December  1999  and  from  1  May  2002  to  1  May  2004  using  MF-DFA  [834],  daily  volatility  of  the  Hang  Seng
China  Enterprises  Index  (HSCEI)  from  2  May  2012  to  27  January  2016  using  MF-DFA  and  MF-CCA  [730],  daily
volatility  of  35  most  liquid  stocks  in  the  Spanish  stock  market  from  June  1996  to  June  2006  using  MF-WT  [764],
trade-by-trade volatility  of  27  highly capitalized individual companies listed  on  the  KOSPI  stock  market in  2008 and
2009  using  MF-DFA  [456],  daily  volatility  of  CAC  40,  DAX,  FTSE,  NASDAQ,  S&P500  and  Canada  TSE  indices
from  2  January  2007  to  31  December  2009  using  MF-SF  [835],  and  daily  volatility  of  the  Casablanca  market  index
and 111 Moroccan family business stocks from 25 March 2013 to 22 March 2016 on the Casablanca Stock Exchange
(Morocco) using MF-DFA [836].
    There  are  also  other  works  dealing  with  cross-correlations  between  volatility  and  other  ﬁnancial  variables  (see
Section   6.5  below).  A  distinct  study  performed MF-DFA  on  the  logarithmic difference of  daily  implied  volatility  for
CAC 40, DAX  and S&P  500 index options from January 2002 to  January 2009 [837].

6.2.2.   Foreign exchange rates
    On  the  foreign  exchange  markets,  researchers  investigated  20-sec  DEM/USD  volatility  from  1  October  1992  to
30  September  1993  using  MF-DFA  [777],   daily  USD/JPY  volatility  from  10  August  2003  to  31  May  2006  using
MF-WT  [716],  daily  CHY/USD  volatility  from  1  January  2005  to  1  November  2014  using  MF-MF  [838],  and  daily
volatility  of  the  onshore  and  offshore  RMB  exchange  rates  (CNY  and  CNH)  from  2  May  2012  to  27  January  2016
using MF-DFA  and MF-CCA  [730].

6.2.3.   Commodities
    The  multifractal  properties  of  the  daily  volatility  time  series  of  several  commodities  has  been  analyzed,  such  as
agriculture futures volatility of Hard Winter wheat from 28 December 1993 to 11 May 2009 and Strong Gluten wheat
from  28  March  2003  to  11  May  2009  on  the  Zhengzhou Commodity Exchange,  and  Soy  Meal  from  17  July  2000 to
11 May 2009 and Soy Bean 1 futures from 15 March 2002 to 11 May 2009 on the Dalian Commodity Exchange using
MF-PF  [839],  WTI  crude  oil  from  13  July  1990  to  6  March  2009  using  MF-DFA  [840],  from  2  January  1990  to  9
March 2010 using MF-DFA [610] using MF-DFA and from 1 January 2005 to 1 November 2014 using MF-PF [838],
Brent  and  WTI  crude  oil  from  20  May  1987  to  14  October  2009  using  MF-PF  [797],  and  gold  from  1  January  2005
to 1 November 2014 using MF-PF  [838].

6.3.   Liquidity measures
6.3.1.   Trading volumes
    Trading  volume  is  an  important  measure  of  market  liquidity  and  the  main  force  driving  price  movements  [841–
844].  For the  US  stock  market, Bolgorian and  Raei  applied MF-DFA  to  daily  S&P  500 trading volume from  1980 to
2009 [845],  Rak  et  al.  used MF-CCA  to  study 1-min transaction number and trading volume of  31 DJIA  stocks from
2008  to  2011  [759],  and  Moyano  et  al.   used  MF-DFA  to  analyzed  1-min  trading  volume  of  30  DJIA  stocks  from  1
July 2004 to 31 December 2004[462].
    For  the  Chinese  stock  market,   Ureche-Rangau  and  de  Rorthays  applied  MF-SF  to  daily  trading  volume  of  36
SSE180 stocks from 9 July 2002 to 9 July 2007 [830], Mu et al investigate 1-min trading volume of 22 Chinese stocks
in  2003  using  MF-DFA  [464],  Yuan,  Zhuang  and  Liu  analyzed  daily  trading  volume  of  SSEC  from  20  December
1990 to 30 December 2010 and SZCI from 3 April 1991 to 30 December 2010 using MF-DFA [718], and Wang et al.
studied 5-min trading volume of the CSI300 index futures from 16 April 2010 to 12 April 2012 using MF-DFA [765].
    On  the  Korean  stock  market,  Lee  and  Lee  applied  MF-DFA  to  1-min  trading  volume  of  KOSPI  from  30  March
1992  to  30  November  1999,  [463],   Lee  used  MF-PF  to  investigate  daily  trading  of  KOSPI  from  November  1997
to   September  2010  [465],   Yim,   Oh   and  Kim   performed  MF-DFA   on  trade-by-trade  trading  volume  of   27   highly

                                                                         85
capitalized  individual  companies  in  2008  and  2009  [456],  and  Oh  adopted  MF-DMA  to  investigate  the  aggregated
trading volumes of buying, selling, and normalized net investor trading [846].
    In  addition,  using  MF-DFA,  Stoˇsi´c  et  al.    studied  daily  trading  volume  of  13  stock  market  indices  (AEX,  BFX,
BSESN,  BVSP,  FCHI,  FTSE,  GSPC,  HSI,  KS11,  MXX, N225, SSMI,  TWII) from 30  August 2006 to  1  March 2014
[708], while Bolgorian analysed daily trading volume of individual and institutional traders on the stocks listed in the
Tehran Stock Exchange from 1998 to 2009 [847].
    In the multifractal analysis of trading volume, the majority of studies in econophysics work on the normal volume
or dollar  volume.  However, the  values of trading volume are  affected by share  split.  Therefore, it  is  recommended to
investigate turnover rates instead of trading volumes [848].

6.3.2.   Bid-ask spread
    The  bid-ask  spread  is  another  important  measure  of  market  liquidity.   Gu,  Chen  and  Zhou  applied  MF-PF  to  1-
min  spread  of  a  liquid  Chinese  stock  in  2003  and  found  no  evidence  of  multifractality  [641].   In  contrast,  Qiu  et  al.
performed MF-DFA  on the  1-min bid-ask spread of 4 liquid Chinese stocks (000001, 000539, 600198, 600663) from
2004 to 2006 and unveiled evidence supporting the presence of multifractality [849].  Obviously, more research should
be conducted on bid-ask spread using different multifractal analysis methods.

6.3.3.   Price gaps on order books
    Price  gap  is  deﬁned  as  the  logarithmic  price  difference  betw  een  the  ﬁrst  two  occupied  price  levels  on  the  same
side  of  a  limit  order book (LOB),  which  is  a  key  determinant of  market depth  and  hence  market  liquidity [850,                         851].
Gu  et  al.   studied  26  A-share  stocks  traded  on  the  Shenzhen  Stock  Exchange  using  MF-DFA  and  found  that  the  gap
time  series  are  long-range  correlated  and  possess  multifractal  properties  [852].   They  also  compared  the  singularity
spectrum width∆αb  of buy LOBs with the singularity spectrum width∆αs  for sell LOBs for all the stocks and reported
a linear relationship between them:
                                                          ∆αs=−0.2223+ 1.1452∆αb.                                                                                                                          (387)
This suggests that the  multifractal properties change across different stocks.

6.4.   Inter-event durations
6.4.1.   Recurrence intervals
    Recurrence  intervals  are  waiting  times  between  successive  events  whose  values  exceed  certain  threshold.  Recur-
rence interval analysis has been performed on ﬁnancial time    series of volatility [234,                       853–   856], return [857–      860] and
trading volume [861–        863].  Recurrence interval is often called return interval [853], which is however not suitable for
econophysics since  the term “return” is  widely used for loga   rithmic price changes.
    The  multifractal  nature  of  recurrence  intervals  of  stock  returns  in  an  artiﬁcial  market  has  been  unveiled  via  the
MF-DFA method [540], in which the return series are generated by an empirical behavioral order-driven model [539].
It  is  found  that  the  singularity  spectrum  width∆α  increases  with  the  Hurst  index  of  relative  prices  of  the  submitted
orders.  However, multifractal analysis on real ﬁnancial ti  me series is rare.

6.4.2.   Intertrade durations
    Intertrade duration is the waiting time between successive transactions.  O´swie¸cimka et  al applied MF-DFA to  the
intertrade durations of 30 DAX stocks from 1 December 1997 to 31 December 1999 [453], Jiang, Chen and Zhou used
MF-DFA  to  analyze  the  intertrade  durations  of  23  liquid  Chinese  stocks  in  2003  [454],  Ruan  and  Zhou  investigated
1-min  intertrade  durations  of  a  Chinese  stock  and  its  warrant  from  22  August  2005  to  23  August  2006  using  MF-
DMA and MF-DFA [455], and Yim-Oh-Kim performed MF-DFA on the intertrade durations of 27 highly capitalized
individual companies listed  on the KOSPI  stock market in 2008 and 2009 [456].
    Heyde  numerically constructed the  activity  time  (transaction time)  of  the  S&P  500  index and  30  DJIA  consistent
stocks from Ito’s formula and used MF-SF to  conﬁrm the multif   ractal nature [457].
    Intertrade duration plays an important role in autoregressive conditional duration model [643], multifractal models
for  asset  returns  [177,    467],  and  Markov-switching  multifractal  models  [104].    These  empirical  results  conﬁrm  the
presence  of  multifractality  in  the  intertrade  durations,  providing  solid  backup  for  the  main  assumption  in  MMAR
models and MSM models.
                                                                            86
6.4.3.   Other microstructure  variables
    Ni  et al.  performed MF-DFA on inter-cancelation durations calculated from the limit order book data of 22 liquid
Chinese   stocks  in   2003  [864],   while   Yue   et   al.     applied  MF-DMA  to   the   time   series   of   the   order  aggressiveness
of  43  Chinese  stocks  (see  [562]   for  the  deﬁnition  of  “aggressiveness”).     These  studies  con     ﬁrmed  the   presence  of
multifractality in  certain microstructure variables.

6.5.   Two time  series:  MF-X
    In  the  last  decade,  some  joint  multifractal  analyses  have  been  performed  on  distinct  pairs  of  ﬁnancial  variables.
Most  of   these   studies   adopt  the   MF-X-DFA  method  [141].     Without  further  justiﬁcation,   the   method  used   in   the
empirical analyses reviewed below is  MF-X-DFA.

6.5.1.   Return-return pairs
    The  multifractal  nature  in  the  cross-correlations in  pairs  of  asset  returns has  been  widely  studied.  For  stock  mar-
kets, researchers have investigated the daily returns of A-share and B-share market indices in Shanghai and Shenzhen
from 2 January 1996 to 1 April 2010 [865], the daily returns of SSEC and SZCI indices from 4 April 1991 to 13 April
2009  [422],  the  daily  returns  of  SSEC  and  SZCI  indices  from  4  January  2002  to  31  December  2008  [108],  the  daily
returns of SSEC and surrounding markets (HSI, NIKKEI 225 and KOSPI) from 1 January 1997 to 31 December 2011
[410],  the  six  daily  return  pairs  of  Amman  AMX,  Egyptian  EGX,  Casablanca  MASI  and  Tunis  TUNINDEX  indices
in  2837  trading  days  from  1  January  2000  [866],  the  daily  returns  of  IBM  and  MSFT  from  1987  to  2012  [867],  the
1-min  returns of  two  pairs  of  German  (CBK-DBK  and  DBK-EON)  using  MF-X-DFA  and  MF-CCA  [109],  the  daily
returns of DJIA and DAX from 12 January 1990 to 12 October 2013 using MF-X-DFA and MF-CCA [109], the daily
CSI  300 spot  and futures returns from 16 April  2010 to  17 February 2012 using MF-X-DMA [868],  the  daily returns
between  market  return  and  6  portfolio  excess  returns  constructed  from  all  stocks  in  NYSE,  AMEX  and  NASDAQ
based  on  the  companies’  sizes  and  book-to-market  (BM)  ratios  from  2  January  1990  to  29  January  2016  [869],  the
daily instantaneous and average instantaneous cross-correlations between the US and the Chinese stock markets [870],
the  daily  returns  of  the  CDS  and  11  sector  indices  in  the  US  stock  market  from  14  December  2007  to  31  December
2014 [752].
    For  oil  and  emission  markets,  scholars have  worked  on  the  daily  returns of  WTI  crude  oil  spot  and  futures prices
from  2  January  1990  to  9  March  2010  [610],  the  daily  WTI  crude  oil  and  Brent  crude  oil  from  3  January  1995  to
13  August  2013  [871],  four  pairs  (CER-WTI,  CER-Brent,  EUA-WTI  and  EUA-Brent)  between  daily  carbon  prices
(Certiﬁcation Emission Reduction CER from 14 March 2008 to 1  5 December 2012 and EU emission Allowance EUA
from 22 April 2005 to 15 December 2012) and daily spot  prices of WTI  and Brent  crude oil [872], three pairs among
daily  time   series   of  oil  (Brent  Crude  Oil   Futures),   gas  (New  York  Mercantile  Exchange  Henry  Hub  Natural  Gas
Futures),  and  CO2  (European Climate  Exchange EU  Allowance  EUA  Futures)  from  22  April  2005  to  30  April  2013
[873],  daily  returns  between  two  crude  oil  prices  (Brent  and  WTI)  and  the  Baltic  Dry  Index  BDI  from  19  October
1988 to  3  February 2015 [874],  and daily  returns of WTI  oil  and  Baltic  Exchange Dirty  Tanker Index (BDTI)  from 4
January 2000 to  4 November 2015 [875].
    The  relationship  between  daily  returns  of  crude  oils  and  stock  markets  has  also  attracted  much  interest,  such  as
WTI and three major US  stock  market indices (DJIA, NASDAQ  and S&P  500) from 2  January 2002 to 29 June 2012
[876],  WTI  and  BRIC  stock  market  indices  (Brazil  IBOVESPA,  Russian  RTS,  Indian  SENSEX  and  Chinese  SSEC)
from 1  January 2002 to  30  September 2012 [877],  WTI  and  ten  CSI  100 sector  indices of  China’s stock  market  from
1 January 2005 to 3  April 2013 [755], WTI  and ten CSI  300 sector  indices from 4 January 2005 to  2 November 2015
[878],  and  WTI  and  six  GCC  stock  market  indices  (Saudi  Arabian  Tadawul  All-Share  Index,  Qatar  Doha  Securities
Market,  Dubai  General  Index,  Abu  Dhabi  General  Index,  Kuwait  Stock  Exchange  Index,  Dubai  General  Index,  and
Oman MSM30 Index) as well as S&P 500 index from 5 January 2004 to 31 August 2013 [879].  A related energy-stock
pair is  the electricity  prices in  the  Indian Energy Exchange and stock  index SENSEX  from 1 April  2009 to  31 March
2014, in  which prices are used as the input∆X  [880].
    The  joint  multifractal  nature  between  crude  oil  prices  and  other  ﬁnancial  assets  has  also  been  studied  and  con-
ﬁrmed,  such  as  two  pairs of  daily  returns of  the  crude oil  (WTI    and  Brent)  and  USD/INR  from  3  January  1995 to  13
August 2013 [871], daily returns of WTI and ﬁve exchange rates (AUD, CAD, MXN,    RUB and ZAR) from 1 January
1996  to  31  December  2014  [881],  daily  returns  of  WTI  crude  oil  and  US  agricultural  commodities  (corn,  soybean,

                                                                          87
oat and wheat) from 3  January 1994 to 31 December 2012 [882], two  pairs of daily returns of the crude oil  (WTI and
Brent) and gold from 3 January 1995 to 13 August 2013 [871], daily returns of WTI versus the 3-month Treasury bill
rate and the  US  dollar index from 4 February 1994 to 29 February 2016 [883],
    Other  pairs  are  daily  data  of  SSEC  and  CNY/USD  from  1  August  2005  to  20  October  2011  [884],  daily  returns
of  Hang  Seng  HSCEI  index  and  RMB  exchange  rates  (CNY  and  CNH)  from  2  May  2012  to  27  January  2016  (MF-
X-DFA  and  MF-CCA)  [730],  daily  data  of  market  fear  gauge  VIX  and  two  Japanese  Yen  exchange  rates  (USD/JPY
and  AUD/JPY)  and  from  5  January  1998 to  18  April  2016  [885],  daily  prices  of  India  SENSEX  and  USD/INR  from
January  1995  to   December  2012  [736]),   daily  returns  of  same   futures  in  the   Chinese  and  US  commodity  futures
markets  (wheat  from  1  November  1999  to  13  December  2010,  soybean  from  4  January  1999  to  13  December  2010,
soy meal from 17 July 2000 to 13 December 2010, and corn from 22 September 2004 to 13 December 2010) in the US
and  China  [886],  daily  returns of  gold  and  USD/INR  from  3  January  1995  to  13  August  2013 [871],  daily  returns of
spot and futures markets of nonferrous metals (aluminum, copper, nickel and zinc) in the London Mercantile Exchange
[887], daily returns between London and Shanghai gold prices from 2 January 2003 to 27 April 2012 (MF-X-DFA and
MF-X-DMA) [888],  daily  returns of  RMB  nominal effective  exchange rate  index (NEER)  and  four  Euronext Rogers
international  commodity  indices  (whole,  energy,  metals,   agriculture)  from  21  July  2005  to  15  March  2016  [889],
5-min  returns  of  Chinese  treasury  futures contracts  and  treasury  ETF  from  6  September  2013  to  26  September  2014
[890],  daily  returns  of  the  3-month  Treasury  bill  rate  and  the  US  dollar  index  from  4  February  1994  to  29  February
2016  [883],  daily  CNY/USD  and  CNH/USD  from  1  May  2012  to  29  February  2016  [891],  daily  data  of  US  gold
price  and  Bombay  Stock  Exchange SENSEX  from January  1990 to  May  2013  [892],  daily  returns of  the  US  interest
rate  (Effective  Federal  Funds  Rate)  and  four  CBOT  agricultural  commodity  futures  (corn,  soybean,  wheat  and  rice)
from 1  January 2000 to  31  September 2014  [811],  and  daily  returns of  Argentina stock  market  Merval  Index and  the
international prices of three commodities (soybeans, corn and wheat) from 1 August 2000 to  20 April 2012 [893].

6.5.2.   Volatility-volatility pairs
    The   joint  multifractal  nature  between   two   volatility   time   series   has   also   been   investigated.     For   stock   market
indices,  some  studies  have  been  done  on  the  pair  of  daily  DJIA  and  NASDAQ  from  July  1993  to  November  2003
[141], the pair of daily A-share and B-share market indices in Shanghai and Shenzhen from 2 January 1996 to 1 April
2010  [865],  the  pair  of  daily  SSEC  and  SZCI  from  4  April  1991  to  13  April  2009  [422]  and  from  4  January  2002  to
31 December 2008 [108], the six pairs of daily SSEC, SZCI, DJIA and NASDAQ from 18 May 1995 to 18 May 2015
using  MF-X-PF  [829],  and  the  pair  of  daily  DJIA  and  NASDAQ  from  2  January  1997  to  29  December  2014  using
MF-X-PF [391].
    Other  studies  consider  the  daily  volatility  pair  of  the  corresponding  agricultural  futures  in  the  Chinese  and  US
commodity  futures  markets  (wheat  from  28  December  1993  to  3  June  2010,  soy  meal  from  17  July  2000  to  3  June
2010,  soybean  from  15  March  2002  to  3  June  2010,  and  corn  from  22  September  2004  to  3  June  2010)  [409]  and
(wheat from  1  November 1999 to  13  December 2010, soybean from 4  January 1999 to  13  December 2010, soy  meal
from  17  July  2000  to  13  December  2010,  and  corn  from  22  September  2004  to  13  December  2010)  in  the  US  and
China  [886],  the  pair  of  positive  series  and  negative  series  of  Leu-EUR  and  Leu-USD  from  June  1999  to  November
2012 [894],  the  three  pairs of  daily volatility  of  crude oil,  gold  and CHY/USD  exchange rate  from 1  January 2005 to
1  November  2014  using  MF-X-PF  [838],  the  daily  WTI  crude  oil  and  US  agricultural  commodities  (corn,  soybean,
oat, wheat) from 3 January 1994 to 31 December 2012 [882], the daily US interest rate (Effective Federal Funds Rate)
and four CBOT agricultural commodities (corn, soybean, wheat and rice) from 1 January 2000 to 31 September 2014
[811],  the  daily  Hang  Seng  China  Enterprises  Index  (HSCEI)  versus  the  daily  onshore  and  offshore  RMB  exchange
rates (CNY and CNH) from 2 May 2012 to 27 January 2016 (MF-X-DFA and MF-CCA) [730], and the daily spot and
futures  prices  of  nonferrous metals  (aluminum,  copper,  nickel  and  zinc)  traded  on  the  London  Mercantile  Exchange
[887].

6.5.3.   Price-volume pairs
    The  joint  multifractal  analysis  of  asset  price  and  trading  volume  investigates  the  temporal  features  of  the  price-
volume  relationship.    In  ﬁnance,  the  price-volume  relation  ship  considers  either  return  and  volume  or  volatility  and
volume.    Analogously,  Wang  et  al.    studied  the  pair  of  5-min  return  and  volume  time  series  of  the  CSI  300  index
futures  from  16  April  2010  to  12  April  2012  [765],  while  Lin  researched  the  pair  of  daily  volatility  and  volume  of
NASDAQ  and  S&P  500  using  MF-X-PF  [383].    We  note  that  the  trade-by-trade  price  returns  and  waiting  times  of
                                                                           88
E.ON (EOA) and Deutsche Bank (DBK) from 28 November 1997 to 31 December 1999 have also been studied using
MF-X-DFA and MF-CCA  [109].
    In  the  econophysics  community,  however,  the  overwhelmingly  largest  number  of  investigations  have  been  con-
ducted  on  pairs  of  return  and  volume  variation,  stimulated  by  the  seminal  work  by  Podobnik  et  al.   on  the  S&P  500
index [861].  Subsequent studies cover hourly returns of day-ahead spot price of electricity and log-difference of trad-
ing volume in the Czech Republic from 1 January 2009 and 30 November 2012 using MF-CCA [895], daily Shanghai
SSEC  index  from  20  December  1990  to  30  December  2010  and  Shenzhen  SZCI  index  from  3  April  1991  to  30  De-
cember  2010  [718],  daily  SSEC  from  20  December  1990  to  30  December  2010  and  SZCI  from  3  April  1991  to  30
December  2010  [718],  daily  data  of  four  agricultural  commodity  futures  traded  on  China’s  Zhengzhou  Commodity
Exchange  (hard  winter  wheat  from  28  December  1993  to  12  March  2010)  and  Dalian  Commodity  Exchange  (soy
meal  from  17  July  2000  to  12  March  2010,  No.   1  soybeans  from  15  March  2002  to  12  March  2010,  corn  from  22
September  2004  to  12  March  2010)  and  four  agricultural  commodity  futures  traded  on  the  Chicago  Board  of  Trade
(wheat from 1 July 1978 to 12 March 2010, soy meal from 1 July 1978 to 12 March 2010, soybeans from 1 July 1978
to 16 March 2010, and corn from 1 July 1978 to 12 March 2010) [408], daily data of the Moroccan stock market index
MASI  from  1  January  2000  to  20  January  2017  [896],  daily  data  of  13  stock  market  indices  (AEX,  BFX,  BSESN,
BVSP,  FCHI,  FTSE,  GSPC,  HSI,  KS11,  MXX,  N225,  SSMI,  TWII)  from  30  August  2006  to  1  March  2014  [708],
daily  data  of  the  50  constituent  stocks  of  the  Nifty  index  of  National  Stock  Exchange  (Mumbai)  from  01  January
2002 to 31 December 2014 [897], and daily data of Au99.99 on the Shanghai Gold Exchange (SGE) from 30 October
2002 to  30  January 2015  and  the  gold futures on  the  Shanghai  Futures Exchange (SHFE)  from  9  January  2008 to  30
January 2015 [898].
    There  are  also  studies  on  price  and  volume  that  are  added  up  before  the  detrending procedure, such  as  daily  data
of copper futures and aluminum futures traded on the Shanghai Futures Exchange (SHFE) from 15 January 2004 to 4
July 2011 and copper and aluminum futures on the London Metal Exchange (LME) [899], daily copper futures series
of Shanghai Futures Exchange (SHFE) from 15 September 1993 to 4 July 2011 and aluminum from 5 October 1998 to
4 July 2011 [900], daily electricity prices and loads in the California electricity market 1999-2000 using adaptive MF-
X-DFA and MF-X-SF [110,             394], and 1-hour and daily prices and loads data of California power market (1999-2000)
and PJM  power market (2001-2002) [901].


7.   Sources of apparent multifractality

    Overwhelming  empirical  evidence  shows  that  various  ﬁnanci  al  time  series  of  assets  and  their  derivatives  exhibit
multifractality.  However, the  underlying physical mechanisms are not clear.  This raises an important and subtle issue
on  the  sources of  the  observed multifractality.  It  is  usually  argued that  the  sources of  multifractality in  ﬁnancial  tim  e
series  are  the  fat  tails  and/or  the  long-range  temporal  correlations  [139].   However,  possessing  linear  correlations  or
long  memory  in  time  series  is  not  sufﬁcient  for  the  emergence    of  multifractality  and  a  nonlinear  process  is  required
to have intrinsic multifractality [499].  Further investigations consider the impacts of linear and nonlinear correlations
on the  observed multifractality [592,          902].
    An  even  more  critical  question  is  to  ask  whether  the  empirical  extracted  multifractality  is  intrinsic  or  apparent.
Indeed, there are many studies showing that empirical multifractal analysis of time series generated from monofractal
ﬁnancial  models  and  mathematical  models can  produce  spurio  us multifractality  [903,                     904].   Moreover, the  estimated
generalized Hurst exponents may deviate signiﬁcantly from    the expected values, especially for short time series [727,
753].  Therefore, statistical  tests  are absolutely necessary in  empirical multifractal analysis.
    It  is  necessary  to  propose  a  standardized  terminology  here  for  a  consistent  picture.   Throughout  this  review,  the
empirical multifractal properties (τ(q) or  f (α)) extracted from time series using multifractal analysis methods is called
apparent multifractality, although it  is  used  to  represent spurious multifractality by some  researchers.  Then, we  refer
to the apparent multifractality obtained from monofractal time series as spurious multifractality.  Time series generated
from  nonlinear,  multiplicative  processes  possess  intrinsic  or  true  multifractality,  whose  nonlinear  component  in  the
apparent multifractal is the  effective multifractality [592].





                                                                          89
7.1.   Spurious multifractality and ﬁnite-size effect
7.1.1.   Bifractality of fat-tailed time  series
     Mantegna  and  Stanley  deﬁned  the  truncated  L´evy  ﬂight  (TLF)       [905],  and  Kopoken  introduced the  exponentially
truncated L´evey ﬂight (ETLF) [              906].  Nakao studied the asymptotical scaling behavior of the structure functions of un-
correlated time series whose amplitudes follow the exponentially truncated L´evey distribution and derived analytically
the mass scaling exponents [672]:                                       {q/γ− 1        for   q≤γ,
                                                              τ(q)=         0                            for   q>γ,                                                                                                                            (388)

where 0<γ≤  2 is the stable  index, and the singularity strength and singularity spectrum are given by
                                                       {1/γ        for   q≤γ,                       {1       for   q≤γ,
                                            α(q)=          0               for   q>γ,             f (α)=0       for   q>γ,                                                                                    (389)

The bifractality also holds for ordinary L´evy processes [671] and time series for which the innovations have a  power-
law distribution with tail  index 0<γ<  2 [673].
     In  addition,  consider  the  class  of  stochastic  process  X(t)  whose  increments  form  a  strictly  stationary  sequence
having heavy-tailed marginal distribution  with  indexγ,  and  which  satisﬁes  the  strong  mixing property with  an  expo-
nentially  decaying  rate  and  zero  mean.   Forγ>  1,  the  scaling  exponents of  the  structure  functions  can  be  expressed
as [457,    907–   909]                             q

                                                        γ,                                                                   if    0<  q≤γ    andγ≤  2,
                                                        1,                                                                    if    q>γ    andγ≤  2,
                                          ζ(q)=         q                                                                                                          (390)
                                                        2,                                                                   if    0<  q≤γ    andγ>  2,
                                                        q
                                                        2+   2(γ−q)2(2γ+4q−3γq)γ3(2−q)2   ,        if    q>γ    andγ>  2.
     For fractional L´evy ﬂights with tail indexγ and Hurst exponent H, the scaling exponents of the structure functions
are [910]                                                    
                                                                 Hq,                                              if    0<  q≤γ,
                                                   ζ(q)=         1+ q(H− 1/γ),       ifγ<  q<              11/γ−H,                                                 (391)
                                                                 0,                                                    if                 11/γ−H<  q,
which depends on both the fat-tailedness and the linear correlations.

7.1.2.   Spurious multifractality
     Bouchaud et al.  built an asymptotically monofractal model and found spurious multifractality through the structure
function approach [903].  LeBron proposed a simple three-factor stochastic volatility (TFSV) model, which is asymp-
totically monofractal, and observed numerically spurious multifractality [710].  Synthetical time series generated from
the  model after  calibrating with  daily DJIA  returns and  the  DJIA  data  share  very similar  structure  functions.  Bianchi
calibrated  multifractional  Brownian  motions  with  the  daily  S&P  500  index  from  20  October  1982  to  27  May  2004
and  the  daily  NIKKEI  225  index  from  04  January  1984  to  27  May  2004 and  found that  the  raw  time  series  and  their
multifractional Brownian  motion  surrogates have  very  similar  multiscaling  behavior  in  the  partition  functions [911].
Similar  results  were  obtained  for  other  six  major  stock  indices  [700].   These  results  show  that  we  cannot  distinguish
monofractality  and  multifractality  in  the  time  series  generated  from  stochastic  volatility  models  and  multifractional
Brownian motion models, and further tests  for nonlinearity are called for.
     Spurious  multifractality  has  also  been  observed  in  the  numerical  multifractal  analysis  of  time  series  generated
from  monofractal  or  bifractal  mathematical  models.     For  instance,   for  L´evy  ﬂights,   the  estimated τ(q)  function  is
close  to  the  bifractal  expression  (388),  but  is  more  or  less  smoothed  around  q=γ,  which  results  in  a  spectrum  of
singularities.     There  are   well-conducted  examples.     For  uncorrelated  time   series  with   a   non-Gaussian  exponential
amplitude  distribution,  spurious  multifractality  is  numerically  observed  [904].   The  linear  absolute  increments  series
of  Devil’s  staircases  show  weak  spurious  multifractality  under  the  local  singularity  method  and  partition  function
approach but exhibit nice monofractality under the punctual singularity method [155].  The linear absolute increments
series  of  fractional  Brownian  motions  show  strong  spurious  multifractality  when  using  the  local  singularity  method


                                                                                    90
and prudent singularity method and relatively weak spurious multifractality when using the partition function approach
[155].
     Furthermore, numerical experiments show that the singularity width of the spurious multifractality depends on the
power  law  tail  exponentγ  and  on  the  Hurst  exponent  H.   For  uncorrelated  time  series  with  q-Gaussian  distributions
that are bifractal, the singularity width∆α increases with decreasing tail exponent and the width values are quite large
in  the  L´evy  regime  under  MF-DFA  and  WTMM  [912].   The  fact  that∆α  decreases  with  increasing  N  has  also  been
observed  in  the  MF-DFA  analysis  of  monofractal  time  series  [561].    For  Fourier  transform  surrogates  of  stochastic
binomial measures, the singularity width∆α obtained from MF-DFA and centered MF-DMA increases with the Hurst
index  H  [291].

7.1.3.   Finite-size effect
     Zhou  generated  random  numbers  of  different  sizes  (N)  from  the  empirical  distribution  of  DJIA  volatilities,  and
different linear correlations with different Hurst exponents (H) were added by manipulating the generated time series
using  the  IAAFT  algorithm  [592].    The  surrogate  time  series  thus  do  not  have  multifractal  nature  by  construction.
Numerical experiments unveil that the singularity width depends on  N  and  H  as follows

                                                                  ∆α(H, N)≈  N−2(1−H)e10(1−H).                                                                                                                           (392)

This  means  that∆α(H, N)→  0  for  long  time  series  and  the  decay  is  faster  for  small  H.   Therefore,  the  linear  corre-
lation  component∆αLM  is  the  outcome  of  the  ﬁnite-size  effect  and  the  linear  correl  ation  component  of  the  apparent
multifractal is
                                                                            ∆αLM=∆αFSE.                                                                                                                                                (393)
     Similarly, for autocorrelated time series with H>  0.5 generated based on the Fourier ﬁltering method [                                                  913], Grech
and  Pamuła  determined numerically the  dependence of∆h  with  respect  to  the  Hurst  exponent  H  and  the  sample  size
N  [914]:
                                             ∆h(H, N)≈  1.206N−0.175(1−  H)+ 0.453N−0.124(2H− 1),                                                                             (394)
or, almost equivalently [915],

                                            ∆α(H, N)≈  1.372N−0.126(1− H)+ 0.572N−0.089(2H− 1),                                                                             (395)

both  of  which  also  suggest  that  the  singularity  width  decreases  with  increasing  sample  size  and  decreasing  Hurst
exponent.  There are corrections to  these formulae when q is not sufﬁciently large [                                       916].
     Overall,  time  series  display  apparent  multiscaling  behaviour  as  a  consequence  of  a  slow  transition  phenomenon
on ﬁnite  time scales.

7.2.   Empirical investigation of different sources
7.2.1.   Impacts of linear and nonlinear correlations (Shufﬂ  e)
     To investigate and distinguish different sources of apparent multifractality, the simplest and most adopted method
is  to  shufﬂe  or  reshufﬂe  the  raw  time  series  and  compare  the  si    ngularity  widths  of  the  raw  time  series  (∆α)  and  the
shufﬂed  time  series  (∆αshuf).   Since  the  shufﬂing  procedure  destroys  any  linear  or  nonli  near  long-range  correlations
while  preserving the  distribution of  the values of  the time  series,∆αshuf  qualiﬁes the  contribution of  the  broad proba-
bility distribution and probably the ﬁnite-size  effect as w  ell  [139]

                                                                           ∆αPDF=∆αshuf.                                                                                                                                              (396)

The impact of correlations on the apparent multifractality is qualiﬁed by [                                    139]

                                                                    Hcorr(q)=  H(q)− Hshuf(q),                                                                                                                              (397)

which can be abbreviated into a  single value [701,                         917]

                                                                      ∆Hcorr=∆H−∆Hshuf,                                                                                                                                     (398)
                                                                                      91
where∆H=  Hmax− Hmin.  Equivalently, we have

                                                               ∆αcorr=∆α−∆αshuf.                                                                                                                                       (399)

where∆α=αmax−αmin.  A  comparison of the values of∆αshuf  and∆αcorr  can determine which source dominates.
    Most  empirical  analyses  reported  a  signiﬁcant  decrease  of  t  he  apparent  multifractality  after  shufﬂing,  indicating
the nontrivial impact of long-range correlations.  Empirical examples contain the daily returns of 29 commodities and
2445 stocks [185], the trade-by-trade returns and the intertrade durations of 30 DJIA  stocks and 30 DAX stocks from
1  December 1997  to  31  December  1999 [681],  the  Iranian  rial-US  dollar  exchange rates  from  24  September  1989 to
15 November 2003 (∆α=  3.54 and∆αshuf=  0.60) [783],  the  1-min WIG20  index returns from 4  January 1999 to  31
October  2005  (∆α≈   0.14  and∆αshuf≈   0.04)  [748],  the  1-min  index  returns  of  S&P  500,  DAX  and  WIG  20  from
May 2004 to  May  2006 [83],  the 20s returns of the  DEM/USD  exchange rates from 1  October 1992 to  30 September
1993 (∆α≈  0.69 and∆αshuf≈  0.34) [777], the 20s volatilities of the DEM/USD exchange rates from 1 October 1992
to  30  September  1993  (∆α≈   2.5  and∆αshuf ≈   0.37)  [777],  the  daily  DJIA  returns  from  26  May  1896  to  27  April
2007  (∆α=  0.22  and∆αshuf=  0.18)  [902],  the  daily  returns  of  the  Indian  BSE  index  from  July  1997  to  December
2007  (∆α=   0.442  and∆αshuf=   0.399),  the  Indian  NSE  index  from  August  2002  to  December  2007  (∆α=   0.558
and∆αshuf=   0.473),  and  the  S&P  500  index  from  July  1997  to  December  2007  (∆α=   0.548  and∆αshuf=   0.328)
[715],  the  daily  returns  of  the  SENSEX  index  from  January  2003  to  December  2009  [735],  the  daily  volatility  of
spot  WTI  and  Brent  oil  prices  from  20  May  1987  to  14  October  2009  (∆α=   0.439  and∆αshuf =   0.142  for  WTI
and∆α=   0.371  and∆αshuf =   0.159  for  Brent)  [797],  the  daily  returns  of  four  foreign  exchange  rates  (JPY/USD,
HKD/USD, KRW/USD and THD/USD) from 1991 to 2005 [772], the daily returns of the Czech PX index from April
1994 to December 2010 (∆α=  0.69 and∆αshuf=  0.44) [296], the daily gold price returns from 1973 to 2011 [788], the
daily returns of the Athens Stock Exchange index, the Irish Stock Exchange index and the Portuguese Stock Exchange
index [918], the daily index returns for the US and seven Asian markets from 1 July 2002 to 31 December 2012 [919],
the  daily  returns  of  the  CSI  energy  sub-industry  index  (ESI)  from  4  January  2005  to  15  June  2015  (∆α=  0.505  and
∆αshuf=  0.427) [756], and so  on.
    There  are  also  examples  for  which  the  reshufﬂed  time  series  h  as  a  broader  singularity  width  than  the  raw  time
series.    For  instance,  it  is  reported  that∆α=   0.773  and∆αshuf =   1.029  for  the  daily  volatilities  of  the  CSI  energy
sub-industry  index  (ESI)  from  4  January  2005  to  15  June  2015  [756],   and∆α=   0.44  and∆αshuf =   0.50  for  the
daily  returns  of  the  Polish  WIG  index  from  June  1993  and  December  2010  [296].    The  observation  that∆αshuf   is
approximately equal  to  or  even  greater  than∆α  should  be  interpreted  with  caution.   The  basic  explanation is  that  the
apparent  multifractality  detected  in  the  time  series  under  investigation  is  independent  of  any  correlation  structures
in  the  data  and  is  thus  fully  determined  by  the  usually  non-Gaussian  distribution.    However,  due  to  the  ubiquitous
presence of ﬁnite-size effects in multifractal analysis, e  ven multifractal time series generated from Markov-Switching
Multifractal  models  may  lead  to  the  same  result  [920].    Hence,  the  observation∆αshuf ≥∆α  per  se  does  not  deny
the  presence  of  intrinsic  multifractality.  One  should  either  perform statistical  tests  based  on  the  IAAFT  algorithm  or
choose a proper scaling range.  Indeed, such an anomaly disappears when the scaling range contains larger time scales
[594].  The shufﬂing test does show that    H(1)=  H(2) is rejected for time series generated from the multifractal model
of asset  returns [921].
    The  adopted  multifractal  analysis  method  could  also  have  impacts  on  the  results.    Caraiani  found  that,  for  the
daily  returns  of  the  Hungarian BUX  index  from  June  1993  to  December  2010,∆α=  0.54  and∆αshuf=  0.34  for  the
MF-DFA method and∆α=  0.49 and∆αshuf=  0.65 for the EMD-MF-DFA method [296].  Although the results of the
shufﬂing test  could be different, there is a  general result s  howing thatαshuf(q=  0)≈  0.5 for shufﬂed time series.

7.2.2.   Impact of  nonlinear correlations (Surrogate)
    A  time  series  has  only  linear  correlations  if  all  the  correlation  structures  of  the  time  series  is  contained  in  the
autocorrelation function, or equivalently, in the Fourier power spectrum.  In this case, its dynamics can be captured by
AR or ARMA  models.  If the correlation structures of a time series cannot be captured by the power spectrum, it  con-
tains nonlinearity.  There are  several methods to  generate linear  surrogates without nonlinearity.  The  most  commonly
used methods for generating surrogates include the Fourier transform algorithm [922], the amplitude adjusted Fourier
transform (AAFT) algorithm [922], and the iterative amplitude adjusted Fourier transform (IAAFT) algorithm [923].
    The  Fourier  transform  surrogate  time  series  are  constructed  to  have  the  same  linear  correlations  as  the  raw  data,

                                                                           92
while any nonlinear correlations are eliminated and the amplitude distribution becomes Gaussian [924].  One performs
discrete  Fourier  transform  of  the  raw  time  series,  randomizes  the  phases  of  the  amplitude  through  multiplying  the
discrete  Fourier  transform  of  the  data  by  random  phases,  and  performs an  inverse  Fourier  transform  to  create  phase-
randomized  surrogates  [922].    The  phase  randomization  approach,  adopted  in  the  multifractal  analysis  of  ﬁnancial
time series, was introduced for the multifractal analysis of heartbeat intervals [925].  Because the phase randomization
procedure weakens non-Gaussianity in time series, the difference HPDF(q)=  H(q)− HFT(q) between H(q) and HFT(q)
is usually argued to  reﬂect the impact of broad distribution  s [803,                    926].  This can also be represented as [701,                917]

                                                                ∆HPDF=∆H−∆HFT,                                                                                                                                      (400)

or equivalently
                                                                 ∆αPDF=∆α−∆αFT.                                                                                                                                        (401)
However, by construction, both the impacts of PDF and nonlinear correlations are removed from the Fourier transform
surrogates.  The difference∆α−∆αFT  contains both PDF  and nonlinearity effects.
     Numerous studies  have  implemented the  shufﬂing  test  and  the    Fourier  transform  surrogate test  to  understand  the
sources of the empirically estimated apparent multifractality in ﬁnancial time series.  The majority of them found that

                                                                 ∆α>∆αshuf>∆αFT.                                                                                                                                       (402)

An   almost   complete   list   includes   the   5-min   stock   returns   for   the   100   largest  US   companies  traded   on   NYSE   or
NASDAQ from 1 December 1997 to 31 December 1999 [927], the daily returns of the Iranian rial-US dollar exchange
rates from 24 September 1989 to 15 November 2003 [783], the daily TEPIX returns from 20 May 1995 to 19 January
2005  [917],  1-min  returns  of  the  TAIEX  index  and  150  stocks  of  the  TSEC  Taiwan  50  and  TSEC  Taiwan  Mid-Cap
100 indices from  January 2001 to  August  2007 [750],  the  daily  return  and  volatility  time  series  of  market  indices for
Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Mexico, Peru, Venezuela and the US  from January 1995 to February 2007 [701],
the  daily  returns  of  172  individual  stocks  included  in  the  Korean  KOSPI  index  from  1980  to  2003  [761],  the  daily
averaging  prices  of  four  Korean  agricultural  commodities  (leek,  radish,  onion,  and  Korean  cabbage)  from  3  January
2001  to  23  September  2009  [810],   the  daily  logarithmic  variations  of  trading  volume  of  the  S&P  500  index  from
1980  to  2009  [847],  the  daily  returns  of  spot  and  futures  prices  of  WTI  crude  oil  from  2  January  1990  to  9  March
2010 [610], the daily exchange rate returns of USD/AUD, USD/EUR and CNY/USD [611], the daily and weekly spot
rates of  VLGCs  on the  benchmark Persian  Gulf  (Ras  Tanura) to  Japan  (Chiba) route  from 3  January 1992 to  24  June
2009 [694], the daily returns of four commodity futures (hard winter wheat from 28 December 1993 to 18 September
2009, soy meal from 17 July 2000 to 18 September 2009, No.  1 soybean from 15 March 2002 to 18 September 2009,
and  corn  from  22  September  2004  to  18  September  2009)  in  China  (it  is  more  complicated  for  the  USA  commodity
futures)  [809],   the  1-min  realized  volatility  of  SSEC  from  4  January  2000  to  31  December  2007  [928],   the  daily
returns  of  CIB-CNY  Composite  Index  (CCI)  compiled  by  the  China  Industrial  Bank  Research  from  21  July  2005  to
30 June  2008 [781],  the  daily returns of  144 Korean equity and  balanced funds from 1  January 2002 to  31 December
2010  [754],  the  daily  returns  of  the  CSI  energy  sub-industry  index  from  4  January  2005  to  15  June  2015  [756],  and
the daily volume changes of the Moroccan MASI  index from 1 January 2000 to 20 January 2017 [896].
     There are  also examples showing that
                                                                 ∆α>∆αFT>∆αshuf,                                                                                                                                       (403)
such as the 5 min returns of the SSEC and SZCI from 4 January 2002 to 31 December 2008 [832], the daily returns of
the world gold prices from 1968 to 2010 [787], the daily logarithmic variations of individual and institutional traders’
trading volume in Tehran Stock Exchange from 1998 to 2009 [847], the daily exchange rates of CAD/USD, JPY/USD,
USD/GBP, DKK/USD, NOK/USD, MXN/USD, BRL/USD, INR/USD and THB/USD [611], the daily returns of CIB-
CNY Composite Index from 30 June 2008 to 18 June 2010 [781], the 10-minute returns of the CSI 300 futures IF1009
from 16 April 2010 to 17 September 2010 [766], the daily spot rates of ships in tanker markets from 27 January 1998
(or  1  July  2004  or  15  July  2005)  to  5  August  2013  [695],  the  daily  returns  of  WTI  oil  and  Baltic  Exchange  Dirty
Tanker  Index  from  4  January  2000  to  4  November  2015  [875],  and  the  daily  returns  of  the  Moroccan  MASI  index
from 1 January 2000 to 20 January 2017 [896].
     The impacts of different sources of apparent multifractality could change for different sample periods.  Zhou et al.

                                                                              93
investigated  the  5  min  high-frequency  returns  of  China’s  CSI  300  index  from  4  April  2005  to  19  January  2012  and
found that∆α>∆αFT>∆αshuf  [612].  They further found that∆α>∆αFT>∆αshuf  for the  period from 8  April 2005
to  16  April  2010  and∆α>∆αshuf>∆αFT   for  the  period  from  19  April  2010  to  19  January  2012,  where  16  April
2010 is the date when the CSI 300 stock index futures were ﬁrst    listed on the China Financial Future Exchange.  Even
more  complicated  situations  are  observed  for  the  daily  gold  price  returns  in  the  Indian  market  and  the  daily  global
consumer price index returns [790,              791].
     In  contrast, the  AAFT  algorithm aimed at  generating surrogate time  series  having the  same  Fourier spectrum and
distribution  as  the  raw  data  [922].   The  values  of  the  raw  data  are  ﬁrst  replaced  by  random  numbe  rs  taken  from  the
normal  distribution  based  on  the  rank-ordering technique.   The  rank-ordered time  series  has  the  same  rank  ordering
as  the  raw  series  such  that  their  ith  elements have  the  same  rank  in  both  time  series.  The  Fourier transform surrogate
of  the  rank-ordered time  series  is  obtained  and  transformed  back  to  have  the  same  amplitude  distribution  as  the  raw
time series through rank ordering.  However, the AAFT algorithm can produce the same power spectrum only for time
series with  inﬁnite length in  the limit    N→∞  and does not usually result in  the same  sample power spectra [923].  To
overcome  this  shortcoming,  the  IAAFT  algorithm  is  introduced,  which  improves  the  AAFT  algorithm  [923].   In  the
IAAFT algorithm, the values of the  raw time  series{x(i)|i=  1, 2,···, N}  are  sorted, resulting in  a  new sequence{yN}.
We  obtain  the  squared  amplitudes{Y2k}  of  the  Fourier  transform  of{yN}.    The  initial  sequence{y(0)N}  of  the  iteration
is  a  random  shufﬂe  of {yN}.   In  the   jth  iteration,  the  squared  amplitudes{Y2,( j)k  }  of  the  Fourier  transform  of{y( j)N}  are
obtained  and  replaced  by{Y2k},  which  are  transformed  back,  and  then  the  resulting  series  are  replaced  by{xN}  with
rank  ordering.  The  iteration  stops  until  a  given  accuracy  is  reached.   For  both  AAFT  and  IAAFT,  the  surrogates still
contains Fourier phase correlations [929].  Unfortunately, the  IAAFT surrogate test  has been applied only in very few
studies  [592,    696,   902,   930].   We  note  that  there  are  also  wavelet-based  methods  to  generate  multifractal  surrogates
preserving the multifractal nature of the raw time  series [931,                   932].
     Similar studies have been conducted on the multifractal cross correlations of pairs of time series, such as the daily
returns  of  copper  futures  and  aluminum  futures  traded  on  the  Shanghai  Futures  Exchange  (SHFE)  from  15  January
2004 to 4 July 2011 and copper and aluminum futures on the London Metal Exchange (LME) [899], the daily returns
of  the  BDI-Brent  pair  and  the  BDI-WTI  pair  [898],  the  daily  exchange rates  CNY/USD  and  CNH/USD  from  1  May
2012 to  29  February 2016 [891],  the  daily returns and volume  changes of  the  Moroccan MASI  index from  1  January
2000  to  20  January  2017  [896],   and  the  daily  returns  of  WTI  oil  and  Baltic  Exchange  Dirty  Tanker  index  from  4
January  2000  to  4  November 2015  [875],  in  which  the  singularity  widths  reduce  for  the  shufﬂed  and    surrogate  data.
There  are  also  cases  in  which  an  even  larger  width  is  observed  after  shufﬂing  or  surrogate,  such  as  the  daily  data  of
market  fear  gauge  VIX  and  two  Japanese  Yen  exchange  rates  (USD/JPY  and  AUD/JPY)  from  5  January  1998  to  18
April 2016 [885], the daily returns of RMB nominal effective exchange rate index (NEER) and four Euronext Rogers
international  commodity  indices  (whole,  energy,  metals,   agriculture)  from  21  July  2005  to  15  March  2016  [889],
and  the  pairs  between  the  steel  warehouse-out quantity  of  three  warehouses  (YZ,  JS  and  BY)  in  China  [430].   There
anomalous results should be interpreted with  caution and further studies are needed.

7.2.3.   Impact of  broad distributions
     For log-normalW-cascades [323], according to Eq. (274), the singularity width is expressed as

                                                                 ∆α=  2√2σ/√ln 2,                                                                                                                                         (404)

whereσ is the standard deviation of the ln(w) random variable.  which is normally distributed.  It follows immediately
that∆α increases withσ.  Oh et al.  performed extensive numerical analysis to validate this relation between singularity
width  and  extreme  values  [772].   Speciﬁcally,  they  generated  multifractal  time  series  fr  om  log-normalW-cascades
with different meansµ and different standard deviationsσ.  The tail exponentsγ of the multifractal data were obtained
using  the  maximum  likelihood  estimator  of  Clauset  et  al.   [933].   The  tail  exponent  is  smaller  if  the  tail  is  fatter  and
there are more extreme values.  It  is found that∆α  is independent ofµ and increases whenγ  decreases.
     To  investigate the  impact  of the  distribution in  empirical studies,  Zhou adopted the  rank-ordered remapping tech-
nique  to  generate  surrogate  time  series  by  replacing  the  raw  data  with  random  numbers  drawn  from  a  prescribed
distribution  while  keeping  the  original  orders [902].   The  algorithm  is  described  as  follows.   For  a  given  distribution,
we generate a sequence of random numbers{z0(i)|i=  1, 2,···, N} [934], which are rearranged such that the rearranged
series{z(i)|i=   1, 2,···, N}  has  the  same  rank  ordering  as  the  raw  series{x(i)|i=   1, 2,···, N}.    In  other  words,  z(i)
                                                                             94
should  be  the  nth  largest  number  in  sequence{z(i)|i=  1, 2,···, N}  if  and  only  if  x(i)  is  the  nth  largest  number  in  the
{x(t)|i=  1, 2,···, N}  sequence  [857,            902].   The  series{z(i)}  is  rescaled  to  have  the  same  standard  deviationσ  of  the
returns{x(i)}:
                                                                z(i)→  z(i)×σ/σz+µ,                                                                                                                                    (405)
whereσz  is the standard deviation of{z(i)}  andµ is the sample mean of{x(i)}.
     Zhou investigated two families of distributions with  fat  tails in  the MF-DFA  analysis of daily DJIA  returns [902].
The ﬁrst  one is a  family of “double” Weibull  distributions

                                                                  p(x)=βxβ−1e−|x−µ|β,                                                                                                                                     (406a)

where the shape parameterβ describes the heaviness of the tails and we require thatβ<  1.  The second one is a family
of Student’s t  distributions                                            )   [                 ] −(γ+1)/2
                                                     p(x)= Γ(γ+1√     2       1+  (x−µ)2                   ,                                                                                                     (406b)
                                                                  γπΓ(γ2 )               γ
which have  power-law tails  with  tail  exponentγ.  Numerical simulations showed that∆α  decreases with  increasingβ
for  the  Weibull  family  and  increasingγ  for  the  Student  family  [902].   Speaking  differently,  time  series  with  heavier
tails  (or   smallerβ   orγ)   exhibit  stronger  multifractality.     Qualitatively  similar   results  are   obtained  for  the   MF-PF
method [592].
     He and Wang investigated the impact of the distribution on the multifractality of SSEC and S&P 500 returns from
20 December 1990 through 7 January 2016 using MF-DFA [112].  The singularity width of the apparent multifractality
is∆α=   0.593  for  SSEC  and∆α=   0.597  for  S&P  500.   When  Gaussian  surrogates  are  used,  the  singularity  width
decreases  signiﬁcantly  to ∆αsurr=   0.294  for  SSEC  and∆αsurr=   0.241  for  S&P  500.   In  contrast,  shufﬂing  the  raw
time series has less impact and∆αshuf=  0.645 for SSEC and∆αshuf=  0.400 for S&P  500.

7.2.4.   Impact of  extreme values
     The  multifractal  behavior  of  time  series  is  inﬂuenced  by  lar  ge  ﬂuctuations,   especially  extreme  events  such  as
market  crashes,  because  the  small  singularities  characterize  large  ﬂuctuations  unveiled  by  large    q  values.   Different
types of investigation have been designed and applied.
     Studies show that removing a single market crash has a large inﬂuence on the singularity width.  Using the structure
function approach, LeBaron compared the multifractal properties of the daily DJIA  index time series sampled from 1
January 1897  to  3  February 1999  and  the  same  sample  with  the  crash  on  19  October  1987  removed [710].   He  found
that  the  removal  of  the  Black  Monday  crash  had  signiﬁcant  inﬂ    uence  mainly  on  multifractal  properties  for  positive
